## In two small books.

->The first /<-

->mind, and what his earnest<-

->how man should walk<-
->in Divine trust.<-

### Jacob Böhme /
->Otherwise known as<-
->The **TEUTONIC PHILOSOPHER.**<- <note>original Latin: "Teutonicum Philosophum". Böhme was given this title because he wrote profound philosophical and mystical works in his native German (Teutonic) tongue rather than the academic Latin of the time.</note>

## Mark 1, verse 15.

> # The time is fulfilled / and
> # the kingdom of God is come
> # near / <term>repent</term> <note>original German: "thut Busse". In keeping with Böhme's theme on the previous page, this refers to a total transformation of the mind and heart.</note> / and
> # believe the Gospel.

<note>This verse acts as the theological anchor for the book. Böhme uses it to emphasize that the spiritual "Kingdom" is not a distant place, but a reality that requires a change in human consciousness ("repentance") to enter.</note>

<summary>This page presents a foundational biblical exhortation from the Gospel of Mark, calling for repentance and faith, positioned as a spiritual preface to Böhme's teachings.</summary>

## Preface
# To the God-loving Reader.

**G**od-loving <note>The woodcut initial 'G' is decorated with Baroque foliage, signaling the start of the author's address.</note> Reader / if you will use this little book <note>original: "Büchlein". Böhme often used the diminutive to express humility or the intimate nature of the devotional work.</note> rightly / and let it be a serious matter for you / you will experience its benefit well / I want to have warned you, however / if this is not a serious matter to you / then leave the precious Names of God / by which the highest Holiness is named / invoked / and powerfully desired / alone / so that they do not kindle the wrath of God within your soul. For one shall not misuse the Holy Name of God: This little book belongs only to those / who would gladly [practice] <term>repentance</term> <note>original: "Busse". In Böhme’s theology, repentance is not merely saying sorry, but a fundamental "turning around" of the soul's will from the self back to God.</note>

<summary>The author opens the preface with a stern warning to the reader, emphasizing that the book's spiritual exercises and the use of God's holy names must be approached with absolute sincerity to avoid spiritual harm.</summary>
<keywords>Preface, Warning, Names of God, Repentance, Devotional practice, Holiness, Sincerity</keywords>

Of True Repentance.

# How a person must awaken the will and spirit within themselves, and what their contemplation and earnest intention should be when they wish to practice effective repentance, and with what disposition they should approach God to seek and receive the forgiveness of sins.

<note>The letter 'W' that begins this section is a decorative woodcut initial featuring scrolled leaf ornamentation, a common stylistic choice in 17th-century theological printing to signal the start of a major chapter.</note> When a person wishes to proceed toward repentance and turn to God with their prayer, they should, before any prayer, examine their own spirit <note>German: *Gemüte*. This term is central to Böhme’s thought, referring to the "mind-soul" or the seat of the will and emotions.</note>—how it stands completely and utterly turned away from God, how it has become unfaithful to Him, how it is directed only toward this temporal, fragile, earthly life, and bears no true love for God or their neighbor. They should consider how it lusts and seethes entirely against God’s commandment, seeking only itself in the transitory desires of the flesh.

Secondly, they should consider how all this is an enmity against God, which Satan, through his deception, awakened in our first parents <note>A reference to Adam and Eve and the concept of Original Sin.</note>; for the sake of this abomination, we must die the death <note>original: "deß Todes sterben." A common biblical phrasing emphasizing the certainty and spiritual weight of mortality.</note> and our bodies must suffer decay.

Thirdly, they should consider the horri-

<sig>A 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: ble</meta> <note>The catchword "samen" completes the German word "grawsamen," meaning horrible or terrible.</note>

<summary>This page begins the formal instruction on repentance, directing the reader to perform a deep internal examination of their alienation from God. It identifies the human spirit's focus on earthly lusts and the legacy of the Fall as the primary obstacles to spiritual health.</summary>
<keywords>True Repentance, spirit, disposition, will, examination of conscience, enmity with God, Satan's deception, mortality, original sin, Jacob Böhme</keywords>

->Of True Repentance.<-

-ble three chains, to which our soul is firmly bound during the time of this earthly life: the first is God’s severe wrath, the abyss and dark world, which is the <term>center</term> <note>original: "Centrum". In Böhme's mystical theology, the "center" refers to the essential core or driving source of a being's life and nature.</note> and creaturely life of the soul. The second chain is the Devil’s desire toward the soul, by which he constantly sifts and tempts the soul, and without ceasing seeks to plunge it from God’s truth into vanity—into pride, greed, envy, and wrath—and by his desire, he constantly inflames and ignites these same evil properties within the soul, whereby the soul’s will turns away from God and enters into <term>selfhood</term> <note>German: *Selbheit*. This is a central concept for Böhme, referring to the state of being turned inward toward the ego and one's own desires, which causes separation from the divine unity.</note>. The third and most harmful chain, to which the poor soul stands bound, is the corrupted and entirely vain, earthly, mortal flesh and blood, full of evil desire and inclination.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;At this point, he should contemplate how he lies trapped, body and soul, in the mire of sin, in God’s wrath, and in the jaws of the abyss of hell; how God’s wrath burns within him in soul and body; and how he is the stinking swineherd <note>A reference to the biblical Parable of the Prodigal Son (Luke 15), who, after wasting his inheritance on a life of sin, was reduced to tending pigs.</note> who has squandered and consumed his Father’s inheritance—namely God’s love and mercy—with the Devil’s fattened sows in earthly lust, and has failed to honor the precious covenant and reconciliation of the innocent suf-

<summary>Böhme identifies three "chains" that imprison the soul: divine wrath, demonic temptation toward "selfhood," and the corrupt desires of the flesh. He uses the metaphor of the Prodigal Son to describe the sinner's loss of their divine inheritance.</summary>

# Of True Repentance.

[suffer]ing and death of Jesus Christ, which God, out of pure grace, has poured into our humanity and has reconciled us to Himself in Him. Also, how he has so completely forgotten the covenant of Holy Baptism (in which he promised faith and loyalty to his Savior) and has so entirely sullied and darkened his righteousness (which God gave him in Christ out of grace) with sins, so that he now stands before God’s face—having soiled that beautiful garment of Christ’s innocence—like a filthy, torn, and ragged <term>swineherd</term> <note>A reference to the biblical Parable of the Prodigal Son, who was reduced to feeding pigs after wasting his inheritance.</note> who has constantly eaten the husks of vanity with the Devil’s sows, and is not worthy to be called a son of the Father and a member of Christ.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Fourthly, he should earnestly consider that grim Death waits for him every hour and moment, and seeks to seize him in this swineherd’s clothing, amidst his sins and <term>abominations</term> <note>German: *Greweln*. This refers to things that are loathsome, shameful, or ritually unclean in a spiritual sense.</note>, and plunge him into the abyss of Hell as a perjurer and a breaker of faith, who shall be kept for the judgment of God in the dark <term>chamber of death</term>.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Fifthly, he should consider the earnest and severe judgment of God, where he shall be brought alive with his abominations before the tribunal, and to him all—

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues the fifth point of meditation begun on page 7, which describes the sinner facing judgment before Christ and those they have wronged. It then proceeds to the sixth and seventh points, focusing on the loss of the divine image and the reality of eternal punishment.</meta>

# Of True Repentance.

Those whom he has offended here with words and deeds, and caused to do evil—so that they also committed sin through his influence—will step before his eyes, curse him, and do so before the eyes of Christ, and also before all holy angels and men. And how he will stand there in great shame and mockery, and furthermore in great terror and eternal despair. And how he would eternally regret that, for the sake of a short time of <term>carnal pleasure</term> <note>German: *Wollust*. In this context, it refers to any worldly or sensual gratification that leads one away from spiritual duties.</note>, he has forfeited such a great eternal salvation and did not take better care of himself, so that he might also be among the community of the saints and enjoy the eternal light and divine power.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Sixthly, he should consider how the godless man loses his noble image (as God created him in His image) and receives a misshapen <term>mask</term> <note>original: *Larva*. In Latin, this refers to a ghost, a specter, or a terrifying mask. Here it signifies the spiritual deformity that replaces the "Image of God" (*Imago Dei*) in a sinner.</note>, like a hellish worm or a loathsome beast, whereby he is then an enemy of God, standing against heaven and all holy angels and men; and how his fellowship is eternally in the gruesome darkness among the devils and hellish worms.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Seventhly, he should earnestly consider the eternal punishment and pain of the damned: how they [stand] in eternal terror, in their [deeds] made here—

<summary>This page describes the spiritual consequences of sin: the public shame of judgment before those one has wronged, the loss of the divine image in favor of a monstrous "larva," and the beginning of eternal punishment.</summary>

<keywords>Last Judgment, carnal pleasure, salvation, Imago Dei, Larva, spiritual deformity, eternal punishment, damnation, repentance</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page continues the seventh point of meditation regarding the eternal punishment of the damned, which began on the previous page. It contrasts the suffering of the wicked with the intended glorious state of humanity as created by God.</meta>

# Of True Repentance.

—who committed abominations shall suffer pain and shall not behold the land of the holy ones for eternity, nor may they obtain any refreshment, as is seen in the case of the <term>rich man</term> <note>A reference to the biblical parable of the Rich Man and Lazarus in Luke 16, where the rich man in hell begs for a drop of water to cool his tongue but is denied.</note>.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Man should earnestly consider and think upon this: How God created him in such a beautiful and glorious image, in His own <term>likeness</term> <note>German: *Gleichnuß*. This refers to the *Imago Dei*, the theological concept that humans are created to reflect God's own nature.</note>, in which He Himself desires to dwell; that He created him for His own praise, and for man's own eternal joy and <term>glory</term> <note>original: *herzligkeit*. In Böhme's writing, this signifies a radiant, heartfelt magnificence or majesty proceeding from the divine.</note>. That he might dwell alongside the holy angels, with the children of God, in great joy, power, and glory, in the eternal light, amidst the song and sound of the harmony of the angelic and divine kingdoms of joy. That he should rejoice with the children of God eternally, without fear of any end, where no evil thoughts could touch him, nor any suffering or sorrow, neither heat nor cold. There, one knows nothing of night, nor is there any day or time anymore, but rather an eternal joy, where soul and body tremble in delight. He should have rejoiced in the infinite wonders and powers, in the beauty of colors, and the adornment of the infinite <term>birth</term> <note>original: *gebehrung*. In Böhme's mystical theology, this refers to the eternal "bringing forth" or "generation" of divine qualities and forms within the wisdom of God.</note> in the wisdom of God, upon the new <term>Crystalline Earth</term> <note>A vision of the perfected, spiritualized world to come, described as being transparent like glass, drawing on imagery from Revelation 21.</note>, which will be as a transparent glass. And [he should consider] how he thus willfully forfeits it all for the sake of such a short, contemptible time—

<summary>This page concludes the meditation on eternal punishment and begins a reflection on the glorious original state of humanity. Böhme describes the intended destiny of man as a harmonious existence within the divine light and the "Crystalline Earth."</summary>
<keywords>Jacob Böhme, Imago Dei, Crystalline Earth, eternal joy, divine harmony, birth of wisdom, repentance, spiritual meditation</keywords>

## Of True Repentance.

...sake <note>This word completes a phrase from the previous page, likely "for the world's sake."</note>; which, however, in this <term>vanity</term> <note>original: *Eitelkeit*. In 17th-century spiritual writing, this refers to the "emptiness," transience, and lack of true substance in the physical world.</note>, in the evil life of the voluptuous flesh, is full of misery, fear, and <term>unrest</term> <note>original: *vnruhe*. For Jacob Böhme, unrest is the natural state of the soul that has not yet found its center in God’s light.</note>, in pure torment. And yet, it happens to the godless just as it does to the pious: as one must die, so also must the other. And while the death of the holy is but an entrance into eternal rest, the death of the godless is an entrance into eternal unrest.

**Eighth**, he should consider the course of this world, how everything is but a <term>trifle</term> <note>original: *spielwerck*. Literally "toy-work" or "clockwork," suggesting the superficial and mechanical distractions of worldly life compared to eternal reality.</note> with which he spends his time in unrest. And he should consider that it goes for the rich and the powerful just as it does for the poor; how we all live and move equally within the **four elements** <note>The traditional Aristotelian elements: fire, air, water, and earth. Böhme taught that these elements make up the fragile, temporary world of "vanity."</note>. The poor man’s morsel tastes as good to him in his toil as the rich man’s does in his worry. We all live by one breath, and the rich man has nothing but a <term>treat for the mouth</term> <note>original: *Mundleckerey*. This refers to fine food or physical delicacies.</note> and the **lust of the eyes** <note>A reference to 1 John 2:16, describing worldly desires that distract from the spiritual life.</note> as his advantage—otherwise, it goes for one as for the other. For the sake of this lust of the eyes, man gambles away such a great <term>blessedness</term> <note>original: *Seligkeit*. This implies both "salvation" and a state of supreme happiness.</note> and leads himself into such great eternal unrest for that reason.

In such contemplation, man will feel within his heart and mind—especially if he constantly models his end before him—that he will [feel] a heartfelt longing and desire for God's **mercy—** <note>The text breaks off here at the word "Barmhertzig-", continuing onto the next page to complete the word "Barmhertzigkeit" (mercy).</note>

<summary>This page discusses the equality of all humans in death and the vanity of worldly pursuits, urging the reader to recognize that wealth and power provide no real advantage over poverty in the face of eternity.</summary>
<keywords>vanity, unrest, four elements, equality in death, lust of the eyes, Jacob Böhme, repentance</keywords>

<header>->Of True Repentance.<-</header>

...mercy! <note>The text begins mid-word, completing "Barmherzigkeit" (mercy) from the previous page.</note> and he will begin to repent of his committed sins, that he has spent his days so poorly and did not notice or consider how he stands here in this world, growing in a field: either as a fruit in God's **love** or **wrath**. <note>In Böhme’s theology, the soul is a plant growing in one of two "principles": the light of God's love or the darkness of His wrath.</note> And he will first realize that he has worked nothing yet in *Christ's* **vineyard**, <note>original: *Weinberge*. A reference to the Parable of the Workers in the Vineyard (Matthew 20), symbolizing the spiritual life and service to God.</note> and that he is a dry branch on the vine of *Christ*. Then, in many a person whom the Spirit of *Christ* touches in such contemplation, great misery and heartache, complaining within themselves, comes over them in abundance over the days of their wickedness, which they have thus postponed and spent in <term>vanity</term> <note>original: *eitelkeit*. Referring to the emptiness and transience of worldly pursuits.</note>, without effect in *Christ's* vineyard.

Now, for such a person, whom the Spirit of *Christ* leads into <term>repentance</term> <note>original: *rewe*. This refers to the emotional and spiritual "remorse" that opens the heart.</note> so that his heart is opened—that he can recognize and repent of his sin—it is very easy to give counsel: He need only put on the promise of *Christ*, that God does not desire the **death of the poor sinner**, <note>A paraphrase of Ezekiel 33:11: "I have no pleasure in the death of the wicked, but that the wicked turn from his way and live."</note> but calls them all to come to Him; He wants to <term>refresh</term> them. <note>original: *erquicken*. To revive, restore, or give rest to the soul.</note> And that there is great joy in heaven over one sinner who repents. Let this person only grasp the words of *Christ* and wrap himself in *Christ's* suffering and death.

But with those I will speak, who...

...indeed feel a desire for <term>repentance</term> <note>original: *Busse*. In this context, it refers to both the internal change of heart and the outward turning away from sin.</note> within themselves, yet can nowhere come to the <term>knowledge</term> <note>original: *Erkantnuß*. This is not just intellectual data, but a profound spiritual realization of one's condition.</note> and to remorse over their committed sins, as the **flesh** <note>In Böhme’s theology, the "flesh" represents the earthly, self-willed nature that resists God.</note> always says to the **soul**: "Wait longer; tomorrow is fine." And when tomorrow comes, the **flesh** says "tomorrow" again; where the poor **soul** groans and stands in a faint, and receives neither true remorse over the committed sins, nor any comfort. To those, I say, I will write of a **process** <note>original: *Proces*. A set of steps or a spiritual method.</note> that I myself have walked: what he is to do and how it went for me. Should anyone desire to follow after, he will experience what is written hereafter.

## The Process of Repentance.

**W**HEN a person finds a hunger within themselves through such above-mentioned contemplations—that they would gladly do repentance, and yet find no true remorse over committed sins within, and nevertheless a hunger for remorse (just as the poor imprisoned **soul** always groans, fears, and must admit its guilt for sin before God's judgment)—they can do no better than this: let them gather their **senses** and **mind**, with all **reason**, together into one, and make for themselves in that same hour, as soon as in the

<meta>catchword: first</meta>

<summary>This page addresses the struggle of the soul against the "flesh," which seeks to delay repentance. The author offers to share his personal spiritual process for achieving genuine remorse and turning toward God.</summary>
<keywords>repentance, flesh vs soul, spiritual discipline, remorse, Jacob Böhme, internal struggle, divine judgment</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the specific mental and spiritual preparations for the "Process of Repentance" started on page 13. It describes the mystical transformation of the believer through the Holy Spirit and the "new birth" within Christ's humanity.</meta>

-># Of True Repentance.<-

[...envelop] his soul entirely in it / so that in his purpose he may attain the love of God in Christ Jesus / and that God, according to His faithful promise, will give him the noble pledge, the **Holy Spirit**, as his beginning / so that he might be <term>born anew</term> <note>Original: *Newgeboren*. In Jacob Boehme’s mystical theology, this refers to the "New Birth," where the soul is spiritually regenerated and Christ is "formed" within the believer.</note> within himself in the humanity of Christ, according to the heavenly, divine essence / and that the Spirit of Christ might renew his mind in His love and power, and make his weak faith strong / also that in his divine hunger he might receive Christ's flesh and blood as food and drink in the desire of his soul / which constantly hungers and thirsts for it / and drink with the soul's thirst from the sweet fountain of Jesus Christ, the water of eternal life, according to Christ's promise and true, powerful assurance.

He should also completely impress upon his mind <note>The original German *einbilden* here carries a deeper meaning than the modern "to imagine"; it refers to the mystical process of forming an internal image or "imaging" oneself into the love of God.</note> the great love of God / that God does not desire the death of the sinner, but wants him to turn back and live. And how Christ so kindly calls the poor sinners to Himself, wishing to refresh them: And that God sent His Son into the world for this reason / to seek and to save that which is <term>lost</term> <note>Original: *verlorē*. The line over the 'e' is a common early modern shorthand for a missing 'n' (*verloren*).</note> / as the poor, repentant, returning sinner / and how He gave His life into death for the sake of the poor sinner.

<meta>catchword: give.</meta>

As noted on the previous page, this is not mere "imagination" but a mystical "imaging" or "in-forming" of the soul into a specific spiritual state or divine reality.</note> / that God in Christ Jesus is much more willing to hear him and receive him into grace / than he is to come to Him / and that God, in the love of Christ, in the most precious Name **JESUS** <note>Original: **IESV**. In Boehme's theology, the "heavenly part" of man became "pale" or "faded" like a dying ember after the Fall of Adam.</note>—that heavenly part / which faded in Paradise through sin / and because of this, He was moved according to His heart / so that He might pour His sweet Love into us / so that the Father's wrath / which was ignited within us / might thereby be extinguished / and transformed into Love / all of which happened for the sake of the poor sinner / so that he might once again obtain an open <term>Gate of Grace</term> <note>Original: *gnaden Pforten*. A common metaphor in this period for the accessible mercy of God.</note>.

In such contemplation, he should firmly impress upon his mind / that in this hour and moment / he stands before the face of the <term>Holy Trinity</term> <note>Original: *Heyligen Dreyfaltigkeit*.</note> / and that God is truly present within him / and outside of him / by virtue of the <term>Holy Scripture</term> <note>Original: *Heylige Schrifft*.</note> / which says: Am I not He, who...

-># Of True Repentance.<-

# A short form of confession before God's eyes.

Each person may form and expand this <term>confession</term> <note>Original: *Beichte*. While this often refers to the formal sacrament, here it is a personal, mystical unburdening of the heart directly to God.</note> according to their own needs, as the Holy Spirit shall teach them; I will only provide a brief guide.

O Great, unsearchable, holy God, Lord of all <term>beings</term> <note>Original: *Wesen*. This term is central to the author's theology, referring to the essential nature or "substance" of both God and created things.</note>, you who have revealed yourself in Christ Jesus—out of great love toward us—with your holy Being within our humanity. I, a poor, unworthy, sinful person, come before your revealed face in the <term>humanity of Jesus Christ</term> <note>Original: *Menschheit Jesu Christi*. The author emphasizes that we approach God through the human nature of Christ, which acts as a bridge between the divine and the seeker.</note>, even though I am not worthy to lift my eyes to you. I plead before you and confess to you that I have become unfaithful and have broken away from your great love and grace which you have given us. I have abandoned the <term>Covenant</term> <note>Original: *Bund*. This refers to the spiritual promise or contract between God and the believer.</note> which you made with me out of pure grace through <term>Baptism</term> <note>Original: *Tauffe*. In this context, baptism is viewed as the moment the soul is formally adopted as an "heir of eternal life."</note>, in which you accepted me as a child and heir of eternal life. Instead, I have led my desire into the vanity of this world and defiled my soul with it, making it entirely bestial and earthly. Thus, my soul no longer even recognizes itself because of the <term>slime of sin</term> <note>Original: *Sünden Schlaim*. A vivid 17th-century metaphor for the corrupting, "sticky" influence of worldly desires that coat the soul and obscure its divine origin.</note>, and it stands before your face as a complete stranger...

<summary>This page provides a template for personal confession, guiding the reader to acknowledge how they have betrayed their baptismal covenant by choosing worldly "vanity" over divine love. It uses vivid imagery to describe the soul's corruption as a "bestial" state covered in the "slime of sin."</summary>

and I do not recognize myself because of **vanity** <note>Original: *Eitelkeit*. Böhme emphasizes that true repentance is an internal transformation performed by the Holy Spirit, not just a human effort.</note> Himself, and intercedes for the soul before God through Christ’s death.

<page-num>24</page-num>

## Of True Repentance.

I will not, however, hide from the dear reader who is of a Christian **resolve** <term>Resolve (*Vorsatz*): A firm spiritual determination or intention to change one's life and turn toward God.</term> how it usually tends to go in such a difficult undertaking—differently for one than for another, depending on how earnest and great the resolve is. For the **Spirit of God** is unbound and tends to follow various processes according to how He knows each person. Nevertheless, he who has been in the war can speak of the battle, so that if it should happen to another in this way, they may be informed.

It happens that such a heart, with strict resolve, comes before God and enters into **repentance** <term>Repentance (*Busse*): In the tradition of writers like Johann Arndt, this is not merely a ritual but a profound internal "turning around" of the soul from the world back to God.</term>. But it happens to him as it did to the **Canaanite woman** <note>A reference to Matthew 15:21–28, where Jesus initially ignores a woman’s pleas to test the strength and humility of her faith.</note>: it is as if God would not hear, and his heart remains without comfort. His **sins** and unworthiness still appear before his eyes, as if he were not worthy; his mind is as if it were mute, the soul groans in the depths, and the heart receives nothing. He also cannot pour out his **confession** <note>Original: *Beichte*. Here referring to the private, internal baring of the soul before God.</note> before God, just as if his heart and soul were locked; the soul would like to, but the flesh holds it captive.

The Devil covers it up tightly and again models the way of **vanity** <note>Original: *Eitelkeit*. The empty, fleeting distractions of the material world that pull the heart away from eternal things.</note> for him, and tickles him with the **lust of the flesh**, saying in his mind: "Wait a while; first do this and that; first gather money for yourself so that you do not have need of the world; then enter into a pious..."

<page-num>25</page-num>

<sig>B</sig>

...life into <term>repentance</term> <term>Repentance (German: *Buße*): In this context, a profound internal "turning around" and spiritual transformation, rather than just an act of penance.</term>; there is time enough.

O, how many hundreds perish in such a beginning, when they return again into <term>vanity</term> <term>Vanity (*Eitelkeit*): The empty, temporary pleasures and pursuits of the worldly life that lead away from God.</term>, and it happens to them as to a young <term>graft</term> <term>Graft (*Pfröpflein*): A young shoot or scion grafted onto a tree; here, a metaphor for the fragile new life of faith in a believer that requires protection to mature.</term> that is broken off by the winds or withered by the heat.

Hear, dear soul: if you wish to become a <term>Knight over Death</term> <term>Knight over Death (*Ritter deß Todes*): A spiritual victor who triumphs over death and hell through faith and union with Christ.</term> and hell in your Savior Christ, and wish for your young <term>graft</term> to become a tree in the Kingdom of Christ and grow, then you must stand firm in your first earnest resolve. It costs your first <term>paternal inheritance</term> <term>Paternal Inheritance (*Vätterliches Erbe*): The original spiritual state and blessings intended for humanity by God the Father before the Fall.</term>, along with your body and soul: [you will become] either an angel in God or a devil in hell. If you wish to be crowned, you must fight; you must triumph in Christ and not lie defeated beneath the devil. Your resolve shall remain firm; you must not prefer temporal honor and goods to this. When the spirit of the flesh says, "Wait a while; it is not pleasant right now," the soul must say: "Now is my time and hour, that I may enter again into my <term>fatherland</term> <term>Fatherland (*Vatterland*): The soul's true spiritual home or the heavenly state from which humanity was exiled.</term> from which my <term>father Adam</term> <term>Father Adam (*Vatter Adam*): Representing the fallen nature of humanity inherited from the first man.</term> led me out. No creature shall hold me back; even if you, earthly body, should be shattered to pieces because of it and pine away, I will even now enter into the <term>Rose Garden</term> <term>Rose Garden (*Rosen-Garten*): A mystical symbol for the spiritual paradise, the presence of Christ, or the state of grace.</term> of my Redeemer **Jesus Christ**, through His suffering and death to Him, my- <note>Original: "mie" with catchword "mei-". This indicates the sentence continues on the next page, likely with "my heart" or "my Savior."</note>

<summary>The author warns against delaying repentance and returning to worldly vanity, using the metaphor of a fragile young plant to describe new faith. He exhorts the soul to maintain a firm resolve to return to its spiritual fatherland, even if the physical body suffers for it.</summary>

I will not hide from the dear reader who is of a Christian <term>resolve</term> <term>Resolve (German: *Vorsatz*): A firm, holy intention or decision to turn one’s life toward God.</term> how it commonly tends to proceed in such a difficult resolve—differently for one than for another, according to how earnest and great the resolve is. ## A Brief Indication,

How the poor soul should step before God again, and how it should struggle for the noble **knightly wreath** <note>The "Ritterkräntzlein" or knightly wreath represents the spiritual crown of victory awarded to the soul that overcomes its lower nature.</note>, and what weapons it should put on if it wishes to go into battle against God’s wrath, and also against the devil, the world, and sin, with flesh and blood, against stars and elements, and against all enemies.

**L** <note>original: "L" is a decorative initial in the source.</note> ove, dear soul, this requires **earnestness**; it must not only be a recital of such words; the earnest, intended will must drive this, or it will not be attained. For if the soul wishes to obtain Christ’s knightly wreath from the noble <term>Sophia</term> <term>Sophia (Greek: "Wisdom"): In Böhme's mystical theology, Sophia is Divine Wisdom, the spiritual bride of the soul who was lost in the Fall and must be wooed back through repentance.</term>, then it must woo her with great desire of love;

<summary>The soul concludes its prayer by vowing to bind the physical body's desires to a holy resolve until death. Böhme then introduces the necessity of spiritual armor and "earnestness" for the soul to win the crown of victory from Divine Wisdom (Sophia).</summary>

As long as they are such, they should not desire this; they will not attain it, and if anything were attained during this time, it is for such people only a **glimmer** <note>original: "glast" — a superficial sheen or reflection, rather than the true light of God.</note> of it. 

But a chaste mind may well attain that the soul be made alive in its noble **image** <term>Image (*Bildnüß*): The original divine likeness of God in which humanity was created, which Böhme believes was "extinguished" or became "dead" in Adam's fall.</term>, which died in Adam—understand, in the heavenly **corporeality** <term>Corporeality (*Leibligkeit*): A spiritual, substantial body or "spiritual flesh" that exists within the soul, distinct from the gross, mortal physical body.</term>, according to the inner ground—and that she may put on the **little wreath** <note>The "Kränzlein" or little wreath is a recurring symbol for the soul's spiritual betrothal to Divine Wisdom (Sophia).</note>, which, though it happens, is nevertheless taken from the soul again and set aside as a crown. 

Just as one crowns a king and afterwards preserves his crown: So it also happens to the soul, because it is still surrounded by the **house of sin** <note>The "house of sin" refers to the mortal, physical body and the fallen nature that clings to it until death.</note>, so that if it were to fall again, its crown would not be defiled. Herewith, enough has been spoken understandably to the children who have known and experienced this; no godless person is worthy to know this further.

# Of True Repentance.

...so let him step forward, provided that an intent is within him, that the person no longer wishes to walk in the old footsteps of vanity—even if the whole world should consider him a fool for it, and even if he should lose honor and possessions, as well as his earthly life, he would still persevere in it.

He must make such a vow to the noble *Sophia* <note>Sophia: The personification of Divine Wisdom, often depicted by Böhme as the soul's spiritual bride and the mirror of God’s glory.</note> in his intent and mind, if he wishes to attain her marriage and love. For *Christ* also said thus:

> Whoever does not leave wife, children, brothers, sisters, money, possessions, and all that he has, yes, even his earthly life, and follows me, he is not worthy of me.

*Christ* intends this regarding the soul’s disposition; that, if anything existed which might hold back the mind in this matter—however beautiful and magnificent it might seem to be in this world—the mind should count it as nothing, and rather let it fall and give it up, than lose the love of the noble Virgin *Sophia* in the blossoming of the Flower of *Christ* <note>The "Flower of Christ" represents the spiritual rebirth and growth of the "new man" within the believer's soul.</note> in his tender humanity within us, according to heavenly **corporeality** <term>Corporeality (*Leibligkeit*): A spiritual, substantial body or "spiritual flesh" that exists within the soul, distinct from the gross, mortal physical body.</term>. For that is the Flower of *Sharon*, the Rose in the Valley <note>original: "Blume zu Saron, die Rose im Thal" — references to the Song of Solomon 2:1, which Böhme interprets as the presence of Divine Wisdom or the Spirit of Christ within the soul.</note>, of which *Solomon* plays, and calls her his dear beloved, his chaste virgin, whom he loved so much, as all the saints before and after him have ever loved her; whoever has attained her has called her his pearl.

# Of True Repentance.

Regarding how one is to pray for this, a short guide follows hereafter; the work itself, however, is committed to the Holy Spirit in every heart where it [repentance] is sought; He Himself fashions the prayer within it.

## Prayer.

> **I**, a poor, unworthy human being, come once more before you, O great, holy God, and now lift up my eyes to you. Though I am not worthy of it, your great mercy, as well as your faithful promise in your Word, has made me bold <note>original: "küne"; here meaning courageous or emboldened by faith rather than arrogance.</note> so that I now lift up the eyes of my soul’s desire to you. For my soul has now grasped the word of your promise within itself, and with this it comes to you. And though it is still an estranged child before you, which was disobedient to you but now desires to be obedient, my soul now winds itself with its desire into the Word that became human, that became flesh and blood, that has broken sin and death in my humanity, that has changed the wrath of <term>God</term> <note>original: "Gottes". In Böhme's theology, the "Wrath of God" is a literal quality or "fire" that must be transmuted into "Light" or "Love" through Christ.</note> into love in the soul, that has taken from death its power and from hell its victory in soul and body, which is an open portal for my soul...

# Instructions /

## How the soul should meet her <term>Beloved</term> <note>original: "Buhlen"; here meaning a spiritual suitor or dear one, without the modern negative connotations of "paramour."</note> / when he knocks at the <term>Center</term> <note>original: "Centro"; the innermost core or spiritual heart of the person.</note>, in the locked chamber of the soul.

**D**ear soul / it must be in earnest / without ceasing / the love of a kiss / from the noble <term>Sophia</term> <note>Divine Wisdom, personified by Böhme as the soul's spiritual bride.</note> in the holy name of JESUS, you will indeed obtain / for she stands even now before the door of the soul / and knocks / and warns the sinner of the ungodly way: If he now once desires her love in this way / then she is willing toward him / and kisses him with the rays of her sweet love / from which the heart receives joy / but she does not immediately lie down in the marriage bed with the soul / that is / she does not immediately awaken within it the faded heavenly image / which grew pale in Paradise / in itself / there is danger with man / for if Adam and Lucifer fell / so it may well happen again / because man still stands so strongly bound in vanity <note>original: "Eitelkeit"; the prideful, transitory nature of the fallen world.</note>.

There must be a faithful bond of your promise / if she is to crown you / you must be tested beforehand / she withdraws her rays of love from you again / and sees / whether you wish to remain faithful / she may well leave you standing / and answer you nothing / not with a single glance

# Of True Repentance.

Wait for her; she will more gladly crown you than you desire it: but you must take care: when the Tempter approaches you with the pleasure, beauty, and glory of the world, then the mind <note>original: *Gemüte*; the inner disposition or the seat of the soul's thoughts and desires.</note> must reject it and say: I shall be a servant in the vineyard of Christ, and not a master; of all that I have, I am only a servant of God over it, and should do with it as His Word teaches me; my heart shall be as simple as the dust <note>original: *albern im Staub*; in this context, 'albern' retains its older meaning of 'simple,' 'innocent,' or 'lowly' rather than its modern meaning of 'silly'.</note> and always humble. Whatever station you may be in, humility must stand at the forefront; otherwise you will not attain her marriage <note>The spiritual union with Lady Sophia (Divine Wisdom).</note>, even though true humility is first born within her marriage; but the soul's free will must stand like a knight: For if the devil cannot prevail through the soul’s vanity, so that she will not take his bait, then he comes with unworthiness, with the **register of sins**; there the struggle begins: In this, Christ's merit must be placed at the forefront; otherwise the creature cannot triumph over the devil, for things go terribly here for many a person, so that even outer reason thinks this person is senseless and possessed by the devil: The devil defends himself so cruelly in some, especially if he has held a great **robber-castle** <note>original: *Raubſchloß*; a metaphor for a heart or soul long-dominated by pride and vice, which the devil treats as his fortified territory.</note> within them, when he is forced to yield and leave his stronghold. Here, one must fight.

<summary>This page advises the soul to maintain humility and act as a servant of God to achieve union with Sophia, detailing how the devil uses both worldly vanity and the weight of past sins to prevent the soul's progress.</summary>

where heaven and hell struggle with one another. Here the Virgin <note>Referring again to Sophia, representing the pure, divine image within.</note>, who reveals herself to the soul out of the precious name of **JESUS**, with Christ the <term>crusher of the serpent</term> <note>original: *Slangentretter*; a reference to the promise in Genesis 3:15 that the woman's offspring would crush the head of the serpent.</note> as the anointed of God, steps toward the soul and kisses it with her sweetest love quite inwardly in its very essence, and presses her love into its desire as a sign of victory; and here Adam <note>The original human nature, now restored.</note> rises from death in Christ according to his heavenly part. Of this I cannot write, for there is no pen in this world for it; for it is the <term>marriage of the Lamb</term> <note>A biblical image from Revelation representing the union of Christ and the soul.</note> where the noble <term>little pearl</term> <note>The "Pearl of Great Price," signifying the seed of divine light and life.</note> is sown—indeed with great triumph, yet at first it is small like a <term>mustard seed</term>, as Christ says.

When this marriage is over, the soul must now see to what it has promised its Virgin, so that the <term>little pearl-tree</term> may grow and increase. For then the devil will immediately come with his storm-weather and with godless people, who will despise and mock this, crying it out as madness. At that point, the person must enter into the <term>process of Christ</term> <note>original Latin: *Proces Chriſti*; the practice of modeling one's life after the life, suffering, and death of Jesus.</note> under His cross ✠ tread—

## A very earnest prayer in <term>temptation</term> <note>original: *Anfechtung*; a technical term for a spiritual crisis or trial where the soul feels assaulted by doubt, the devil, or a sense of God's abandonment.</note>, against God's wrath in the conscience, also against flesh and blood, when the tempter approaches the soul and wrestles with it.

> **O** most profound love of God in Christ Jesus, do not forsake me in this distress; I am indeed guilty of the sins which now rise up in my conscience; if you forsake me, I must sink. You have indeed promised me in your Word: "Can a mother <note>A reference to Isaiah 49:15: "Can a woman forget her sucking child... yet will I not forget thee."</note>..."

# Of True <term>Repentance</term> <note>original: *Buße*; here meaning a total inward turning toward God.</note>.

...[even if a mother] should forget her child, which would surely be a painful thing, yet you will not forget me; you have inscribed me in your hands, in your hands pierced through with sharp nails, and in your hollow side, from which blood and water ran <note>The "hollow side" refers to the spear-wound in Christ's side, which mystics like Böhme viewed as a literal doorway for the soul to enter into God's heart.</note>, you have inscribed me. I, a poor human, seized in your wrath, can do and am capable of nothing now before you; I only sink myself into your wounds and death.

O great <term>mercy</term> <note>original: *Barmhertzigkeit*</note> of God, deliver me from the devil’s bonds: I have otherwise no refuge in anything, but only in your holy wounds and death. Into you I sink in the anguish of my conscience; do with me as you will. In you I will now live or die, as you will; only let me die and perish in your death; bury me only in your death, so that the anguish of hell does not touch me. Why should I excuse myself before you, who test my heart and kidneys <note>original: *Hertze vnd Nieren*; a biblical idiom (as in Psalm 7:9) meaning God searches the most secret parts of a person's mind and emotions.</note>, and set my sin before my eyes? I am indeed guilty of it, and surrender myself to your judgment; carry out your judgment over me through the death of my Savior <term>Jesus Christ</term>.

O merciful **God** in Christ **Jesus** / I pray to you in my understanding / out of the depth of your love toward us poor humans / which you have revealed in me / according to the <term>hidden man</term> <note>A reference to the "inner man" or the spiritual soul that is reborn within the physical body.</note> / call us all in you to yourself. ## Thereupon the noble *Sophia*
## responds to the Soul, and says:

**M**y dear <term>beloved</term> <note>original: Buhle; a term for an intimate partner or suitor, used here for the mystical relationship between the soul and Wisdom.</note> and faithful treasure, you rejoice me greatly in your beginning. I have indeed broken through the deep gates of God to reach you—through God’s wrath, through Hell and Death, into the house of your misery—and have given you my love out of grace, and released you from the chains and bonds to which you were tightly bound. I have kept my faithfulness to you. But you now ask something difficult of me, which I am reluctant to venture with you. You wish to have my little Pearl as your own possession. Consider, my dear bridegroom, how you previously neglected it in <term>Adam</term> <note>Referring to the Fall of Man, where humanity lost its original divine likeness and the "Pearl" of wisdom.</note>? Furthermore, you still stand in great danger, and walk in two perilous realms: first, in your <term>fire-origin</term> <note>original: fewer-vrstand; the fundamental, energetic root of the soul, which Böhme describes as a "consuming fire" if not tempered by divine love.</note>, you walk in the land where God calls Himself a strong, jealous **GOD** and a consuming fire.

In the other realm, you walk in the outer world in the air, in vain, corrupting—

...[earthly] flesh and blood: where the world's pleasure, along with the Devil's attacks, rushes over you every hour. In your great joy, you might introduce <term>earthliness</term> <note>original: Iridigkeit; the state of being bound to the material, fallen world.</note> once again into my beauty and darken my Pearl for me. You might also become proud, as <term>Lucifer</term> <note>The fallen angel who, in Böhme's theology, fell because he wanted to be his own master rather than remaining in God's harmony.</note> did when he held the Pearl as his own possession, and you might turn away from God's Harmony; thus, I would thereafter be eternally robbed of my <term>beloved</term> <note>original: Buhle; an intimate term for a lover, used here for the mystical bond between Wisdom and the Soul.</note>.

I will keep my Pearl within me, and I will dwell in Heaven within you—in your faded inner humanity which has now been made alive again in me. I will reserve my Pearl for <term>Paradise</term> <note>The spiritual state of perfection that exists beyond the material world.</note> until you lay aside this earthliness. Then I will give it to you as your own possession. 

But my countenance and the sweet rays of the Pearl I will gladly offer to you during the time of this earthly life. I will dwell with the Pearl in the <term>inner choir</term> <note>original: inneren Chor; refers to the "sanctuary" or the most holy, innermost part of the human spirit where God resides.</note> and be your faithful, dear bride. I do not marry myself into your earthly flesh, for I am a Queen of the Heavens, and my kingdom is not of this world. However, I will not cast away your outward life, but will often visit it with my <term>rays of love</term> <note>original: Liebe-Stralen; the comforting and enlightening influence of the Holy Spirit.</note>. For your outward humanity shall return <note>A reference to the resurrection of the body.</note>. ...[earthliness] <note>The text begins with the suffix "-keit," continuing from the final word of the previous page.</note> I will not have: God also did not create this so gross and earthly in <term>Adam</term> <note>Referring to the first man before the Fall; Böhme views Adam's original state as spiritual rather than material.</note> out of His own purpose; rather, your desire has grasped this <term>beastly coarseness</term> <note>original: Viehische Grobheit; the animal-like, heavy, and mortal nature of the human body after the Fall.</note> in Adam through lust, out of and with all the essences of awakened <term>vanity</term> <note>original: Eitelkeit; here meaning the fragility, corruption, and emptiness of the material world.</note>—an earthly property in which heat and cold, as well as pain and enmity, and even breaking, consist.

You must work and bring forth in this time. You are the <term>root of this tree</term>; from you, branches shall be born, all of which must be born in anguish. But I press through your branches with their sap and bear fruit upon your boughs, and you know it not. For the Most High has ordained me thus, to dwell with and within you.

Therefore, wrap yourself in patience, and guard yourself against the <term>lust of the flesh</term>; break its will and desire, hold it in check like an <term>unruly horse</term> <note>original: böses Roß; a metaphor for the undisciplined physical body and its impulses.</note>; then I will often [visit/strengthen] you in your...

You shall remain standing in <term>resignation</term> <note>original: Gelassenheit; a central concept in German mysticism meaning a state of yieldedness, inner stillness, and the total surrender of the self-will to God.</note>, and hear what the Lord plays within you in your <term>harmony</term> <note>original: Harmony; the inner spiritual accord or "divine music" created when the soul is aligned with God's will.</note>. But the pearl crown, with which I crowned you, I have set aside; you shall wear it no more until you are pure before me.

### III.

## The Soul speaks further to the noble Sophia.

**A**h, you my beautiful and sweet <term>consort</term> <note>original: Gemahlin; meaning spouse or bride, reflecting the mystical tradition of the "spiritual marriage" between the soul and Divine Wisdom.</note>, what shall I say before you? Only let me be commended to you; I cannot preserve myself. Will you not now [give] me the
->pearl<-

<meta>catchword: pearl</meta>
<meta>signature: th i</meta>

<summary>Divine Wisdom (Sophia) promises the soul a temporary wreath of victory but explains that the permanent crown of pearls must be withheld until the soul is fully purified. The Soul responds by surrendering its will to Sophia, acknowledging its own inability to protect itself.</summary>

## The First Chapter.

<margin>14</margin>
**W**e have a true example in Lucifer, and also in Adam, the first man, of what **Selfhood** <term>Selfhood (Selbheit): The state of the fallen ego that seeks to be its own master and center, independent of God.</term> does when it **receives the Light of Nature as a possession**, so that it may walk in its own governance through the understanding. <margin>H</margin> We see this also in the scholarly and clever <note>original: Kunst-gelehrten; refers to those skilled in worldly arts and sciences</note> people who, when they obtain the **light of the outer world or nature** into a **rational possession**, produce nothing but their own pride <note>original: Hoffart; a strong term for spiritual arrogance or vanity</note>. Yet all the world seeks and desires this so intensely as the greatest treasure; and indeed it is the best treasure of this world, if it is used correctly.

But because **Selfhood**, as **Reason**, is held in a heavy prison—both in **God’s wrath** and in **earthliness**—and stands firmly bound, it is very dangerous for man to carry the **light of knowledge** within **Selfhood** as a **possession of the self.**

For the **wrath** <note>original: Grim; refers to the fierce, "dark" quality of nature when separated from God's light</note> **of eternal and temporal nature** soon takes delight in this, whereby **Selfhood and one's own reason** rise up in **pride** and turn away from true, **resigned humility** toward **GOD**—

<sig>D</sig>
<meta>catchword: breaks/</meta>

<summary>Böhme explains that natural intelligence and knowledge become dangerous "possessions" when claimed by the ego (Selfhood), leading to pride rather than the humble resignation required for spiritual life.</summary>
<keywords>Selfhood, Light of Nature, pride, God's wrath, Lucifer, Adam, reason, humility, resignation, spiritual knowledge</keywords>

This transition marks the shift from resigned humility to the prideful desire for self-governance.</meta>

<page-num>74</page-num>
<header>On True Resignation.</header>

...breaks off and no longer wishes to eat from the **Fruit of Paradise** <term>Fruit of Paradise (Paradyßfrucht): The spiritual nourishment or "food" of the divine realm, representing life lived in harmony with God's will.</term>, but rather from the property of **Selfhood** <term>Selfhood (Selbheit): The state of the fallen ego that seeks to be its own master and center, independent of God.</term>, as if from the governance of life in which good and evil reside. This is just as Lucifer and Adam did, both of whom—through the desire for **Selfhood**—entered back into the **Primal State** <term>Primal State (Urstand): The original, unmanifested source or ground from which all things were created.</term> from which the **Creature** <term>Creature (Creature): Any created being, whether an angel or a human.</term> was born, and stepped into a created state.

Lucifer entered into the **Center** <note>original: Centrum; in Böhme's thought, the "Center" is the deep, internal core of a being's nature, which can be either dark/fiery or light/divine.</note> and the fierce nature, into the **Matrix of Fire** <note>original: Matrix; Böhme uses this Latin term to mean a "womb" or a generative source from which a specific quality of life is born.</note>, and Adam into the earthly nature, into the **Matrix of the outer world**, namely into the desire for good and evil.

This happened to both of them for the reason that they held the light of understanding within **Selfhood**. Within this light, they could mirror and behold themselves, whereby the spirit of **Selfhood** entered into the **Imagination** <term>Imagination (Imagination): Not mere "make-believe," but the soul's active power to "image" or pull its desires into a concrete reality.</term>, as into a desire for the **Center**, in order to elevate itself, to become great and powerful, and moreover to become more clever. 

Just as Lucifer sought the Mother of Fire in his **Center**, intending thereby to rule over God’s love and all that is angelic; and Adam also desired to test the Mother from which good and evil spring within the **Essence** <term>Essence (Essentz): The underlying spiritual substance or "stuff" of a being.</term>, and led his desire into it through his will, so as to become clever and wise. Both of whom—Lucifer and also Adam—in their false desire, [were] in the Mother
<meta>catchword: captured (gefangen)</meta>

Böhme describes this as a shift from eating the Fruit of Paradise to seeking self-centered power and cleverness.</summary>

**Light from God**. But no; into this—namely, into the **conceit** of **selfhood**, into the **external light of reason**—the **devil** swings himself back with a sevenfold desire, after he had been forced to retreat from the first light, which is Divine. Concerning which Christ said:

> When the unclean spirit goes out of a person, he wanders through dry places, seeking rest, and finds none. Then he takes seven spirits with him, who are worse than himself, and returns to his first house, and finds it swept and adorned, and then lives there, and it becomes worse for that person than before, etc. <note>A paraphrase of Matthew 12:43–45. Böhme interprets the "swept house" as a mind cleaned of vice but filled with intellectual pride.</note>

The "adorned house" is the **light of reason** within **selfhood**. For when a person directs their desire and will into God, and enters into abstinence from their evil life, and desires God's love, then that same love appears to them with its very friendly, joy-filled gaze, through which the **external light of reason** is also ignited. For wherever **God’s light** is kindled, everything becomes bright. There the **devil** cannot remain; he must depart from there. Then he searches through the <term>Origin</term> <note>original: Vrstand; in Böhme's vocabulary, this refers to the primal source or the fundamental grounding of life’s forces.</note> of the mother of life, namely, the <term>Center</term> <note>original: Centrum; the inner "engine" of the soul where spiritual forces are balanced or conflicted.</note>, but it has become a dry, powerless place. <page-num>72</page-num>
# ## ->On True Resignation.<-

Although the **stars** <note>original: Gestirne; refers to the astral influences of the physical universe upon the human soul and mind.</note> cannot grasp the **Spirit of God**, they much prefer a **house of light** in which to take pleasure, rather than a locked house where they have no permanence.

Thus, this person then walks as if they had become **drunk** in the stars; they grasp great and wondrous things and have a constant guide in the stars. And the **Devil** notices quite well where a gate stands open for him, where he may ignite the <term>Center of life</term> <note>original: lebensCentrum; the spiritual and energetic core of an individual's existence.</note>, so that the **will-spirit** ascends in its own **pride**, in its conceit (or indeed in greed). From this arises **self-honor**, for the **will of reason** wants to be honored. For it thinks it has the **prize of salvation** <note>original: Braten deß heils; literally "the roast of salvation," an idiom for having secured the best part or the essence of something.</note> because it possesses the **light of reason**, and it presumes to judge the "locked house" <note>Refers to the mysteries of God or the souls of others that remain hidden to mere human intellect.</note>, which God alone may truly unlock.

He thinks honor is now due to him because he has attained this **intellectual understanding**, and he never notices how the **Devil** takes pleasure with his desire in his <term>seven forms of life</term> <note>original: Sieben Lebens gestalten; Böhme’s foundational concept of seven qualities that constitute all of reality.</note> of the <term>Center of nature</term>, and what kind of horrible errors he brings about.

From this understanding, the **false <term>Babel</term>** <note>In Böhme’s work, "Babel" represents the "confused" institutional church that relies on human reason and dogma rather than direct spiritual experience.</note> was born into the **Christian Church** on earth, where one teaches and governs with **conclusions of reason**, and the child of **drunkenness** with <term>selfhood</term> <note>original: Selbheit; the state of being centered on the ego rather than on God.</note> and ow[nness]...

# On True Resignation.

-># On True Resignation.<-

The beginning should be: Man should not govern his life solely by the outer <term>light of reason</term> <note>original: vernunfft Licht; refers to human intellectual power, logic, and natural understanding.</note>; that is indeed good, but he should sink himself with that same reason into the deepest humility before God, and place the Spirit and will of God at the forefront of all his searching, so that the light of reason may see through God’s light. And though reason may recognize much, it should not claim this for itself as its own <term>property</term> <note>original: Eygenthumb; in this context, it refers to the spiritual pride of claiming divine insights as one's own personal achievement.</note>, but rather give the glory to God, to whom alone knowledge and wisdom belong.

For the more reason sinks into <term>simple</term> <note>original: albere; in 17th-century spiritual texts, this means "innocent" or "guileless" rather than the modern sense of "foolish."</note> humility before God, and the more unworthy it considers itself before God, the more it dies to its own desire, and the more God’s Spirit penetrates it and leads it into the highest knowledge, so that it may behold the great <term>wonders of God</term> <note>original: Wunder Gottes; the hidden mysteries of the divine nature and the created world.</note>. For God’s Spirit enters only into <term>resigned</term> <note>original: gelaſſenen; the state of being yielded or surrendered in the spirit of "Gelassenheit."</note> humility, which does not seek or desire itself: That which in itself desires to be <term>simple</term> <note>original: einfältig; single-minded or pure in focus toward God.</note> before God, <term>that</term> <note>abbreviation for "das"</note> the Spirit of God takes hold of, and carries out in His wonders. Only those who fear and bow before Him please Him. For **God** has not created us for <term>selfhood</term> <note>original: Eygenheit; the state of acting out of one's own ego or selfish interest rather than for God.</note>, but as instruments of His wonders, through which He wishes to reveal His wonders Himself. ...and hopes for all good from Him: But the **self-will** <note>original: eygene Wille; the human will acting independently of divine guidance.</note> governs itself, for it has broken itself off from God.

Everything that self-will does is **sin**, and against God: For it has departed from the **order** in which God created it, moving into a state of disobedience, and wants to be its own master. When self-will dies to <term>selfhood</term> <note>original: Selbheit; the ego-centric state of being focused on one's own desires and identity separate from God.</note>, it is then free from sin: For it desires nothing except only this: what God desires from His creature; it desires only to do that for which God created it, and what God wills to do through it. And although it indeed is the doing, and must be so, it is nevertheless only the **tool** <note>original: Werckzeug; an instrument or vessel used by a higher power.</note> of the doing, with which God does what He wills.

For that is precisely the right **faith** in man: that he dies to selfhood—that is, to his own desire—and introduces his desire in all his beginnings and undertakings into God’s will, and takes no credit for any individual action, but in all his doing regards himself only as God's servant and minister, and thinks that everything he does and intends, he does for God: For in such an undertaking, the Spirit leads him into true **faithfulness** and **integrity** <note>original: Redligkeit; refers to uprightness, sincerity, and moral honesty in dealings with others.</note> toward his neighbor; for he thinks: I do my work not for myself, but for my God,

-># On True Resignation.<-

...[called] Divine, if it does not walk and work within God’s will.

> All plants, says Christ, which my Father has not planted, shall be uprooted and burned in the fire. <note>A reference to Matthew 15:13.</note>

All the works of man which he performs outside of God’s will shall all burn in the final fire of God, and be given over to the wrath of God—as if to the abyss of darkness—for its own gratification. For Christ says:

> Whoever is not with me is against me, and whoever does not gather with me scatters. <note>A reference to Matthew 12:30.</note>

Whoever does not work and act in a <term>resigned</term> <note>original: gelassenen; the root of "Gelassenheit." It describes a soul that has let go of its own desires to become a vessel for God.</note> will, in trust upon Him, only wastes and scatters; it is not pleasing to Him. Nothing pleases **GOD** except what He Himself wills through His Spirit and performs through His instrument <note>Meaning the human being acting as a tool for the divine.</note>.

Therefore, everything is but a fable and <term>Babel</term> <note>Böhme uses "Babel" to symbolize the "City of Confusion"—the prideful, human-constructed systems of logic and religion that are not founded on direct spiritual experience.</note> which occurs through the conclusions of human <term>selfhood</term> <note>original: Selbheit; the self-centered state of the soul that acts independently of God.</note> regarding Divine knowledge and will. It is merely a work of the <term>stars</term> <note>original: Gestirn; Böhme believed the physical mind and the natural world were heavily influenced by "astral" or cosmic forces which must be transcended to reach the divine.</note> and the external world, and is not recognized by **GOD** as His work. Instead, it is a play of the <term>wrestling wheel of nature</term> <note>original: ringenden Rades der Natur; a concept describing the perpetual, churning conflict of opposing forces (like light and dark, or heat and cold) that drive the material world.</note>, where good and evil wrestle with one another. What the good builds, the evil breaks; and what the evil builds, the good breaks. And this is the great misery of the perish-

<summary>Böhme explains that human actions originating from the "self" are spiritual failures, comparing them to "Babel" and the "wrestling wheel of nature" where opposing forces fruitlessly cancel each other out.</summary>

# On True Resignation.

...[given] hardship, all of which belongs to the judgment of God and to the sorting out of strife.

Therefore, whoever works and builds much in such hardship works only for the judgment of God: for there is nothing perfect or permanent there; it must all enter into <term>putrefaction</term> <note>original: putrefaction; an alchemical term for the process of decay or breaking down. In Böhme's theology, the "old" self or work must rot away so that a new, spiritual form can be born.</note> and be separated. For what is worked in <term>God's wrath</term> <note>original: Gottes Zorn; in Böhme’s thought, the "wrath" is the dark, consuming fire of nature that has not yet been lightened by God's love.</note> is taken in by Him, and shall keep its desire in the <term>Mystery</term> <note>original: Mysterio; referring to the "Mysterium Magnum," the hidden source or "chaos" where all things are stored until the end of time.</note> until the Day of Judgment of God, when evil and good shall be separated.

But if man now turns around, and departs from **selfhood**, and enters into God’s will, then also the good which he has worked in **selfhood** shall be released from the evil he has worked. For <term>Isaiah</term> <note>original: Esaias.</note> says: 

> Though your sins be blood-red, if you turn back and do penance, they shall become white as snow, like wool. <note>A reference to Isaiah 1:18.</note>

For the evil is swallowed up in the wrath of God into death, and the good goes forth as a growth 
->out of the wild earth.<-

<summary>Böhme explains that works created through human ego and struggle must undergo spiritual decay (putrefaction) to be sorted by God, but through repentance and leaving "selfhood," a person's good deeds can be redeemed from their past errors.</summary>

## Chapter II.

**W**hoever <note>The original German text begins with a decorative woodcut initial 'W', common in 17th-century printing to mark the start of a significant chapter.</note> intends to perform something perfect and good, in which he hopes to rejoice eternally and to enjoy its fruit, should depart from <term>selfhood</term> <note>original: Selbheit; refers to the ego-driven state of being where a person acts out of their own selfish desires rather than God's will.</note>—as if from a narrow and cramped desire—and enter into <term>resignation</term> <note>original: Gelassenheit; a core concept in Böhme’s philosophy meaning a state of yieldedness, surrender, or "letting go" to allow the divine to act through the individual.</note> in <term>God's Will</term>, and work in union with God. Even if the earthly desire of selfhood still clings to him in flesh and blood, as long as the soul’s will does not embrace or "take in" that desire, then selfhood can produce no work. For the resigned will continually breaks the essence of selfhood again, so that the <term>Zorn Gottes</term> <note>translated: God's wrath; Böhme views "wrath" as the dark, fiery center of nature that must be overcome by light and love.</note> cannot reach it. And if the wrath does reach it—which is not entirely impossible and may indeed happen—the resigned will nevertheless carries its power upward through the wrath; thus it stands as a figure before God, like a work of victory within the "Wonder" <note>original: Wunder; refers to the divine mystery or the manifestation of God's glory in the created world.</note>, and may inherit <term>divine adoption</term> <note>original: Kindschafft; literally "childship," referring to the spiritual state of being a child of God.</note>. 

Therefore, it is not good to speak or act when <term>Reason</term> <note>original: Vernunfft; Böhme often uses this term to describe human intellectual logic, which he considers blind to spiritual truths unless illuminated by God.</note> is ignited in the desire of selfhood. Such a desire works instead within God's wrath, which will cause the person harm, for his work will be drawn into God's wrath and kept until the great <term>Day of Judgment</term> of God.

All false desire, by which a person [seeks] the multiplicity of the world from his neighbor

...intends to draw things to himself with cunning, to the ruin of his neighbor, all of it will be consumed by **God's wrath** and belongs to the judgment, where all things shall be made manifest. There, within the <term>Mystery</term> <note>original: Mysterio; in Böhme's thought, the "mysterium" is the hidden, spiritual core of reality where all things are known in their true state.</note> of the <term>revelation</term> <note>original: offenbarung; the process by which God's hidden nature becomes visible in the created world.</note>, every power and <term>essence</term> <note>original: Wesen; refers to the fundamental substance or "being" of a thing.</note>, in both good and evil, shall rise before the eyes of everyone. For God has introduced his active power into an essence for this very reason: so that God’s love and wrath might be revealed, serving as an example to God's honor and his wondrous deeds.

And every <term>creature</term> <note>original: Creatur; any created being, from humans to animals to spiritual entities.</note> is to know this: that it should remain in that state into which God has created it. Otherwise, it runs into the opposition and enmity of the will of God and leads itself into torment. For no creature that has been created into the <term>darkness</term> <note>original: Finsternuß; Böhme uses this to describe the "dark world" or the root of nature which lacks the light of divine love.</note> suffers pain from that darkness. Just as a poisonous worm feels no pain from its own poison—for the poison is its very life—yet if it were to lose its poison and something "good" were introduced into it and became manifest in its <term>essence</term> <note>original: Essentz; the spiritual quality or "flavor" that defines a being's character.</note>, that would be its pain and death. In the same way, evil is the pain and death of the good.

Böhme uses the metaphor of a poisonous worm to illustrate that suffering occurs when a creature acts against its inherent nature or God's intended order.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the divine order and the manifestation of God's power. It transitions from the previous discussion on how every creature must face judgment to the specific origin of humanity and the fallen state of the Devil, illustrating how being out of harmony with one's original nature results in suffering.</meta>

<page-num>94</page-num>
# ->On True Resignation.<-

Human beings were created into Paradise, into **God's** love; but if they lead themselves into wrath—as if into a poisonous torment and death—then this contrary life becomes an agony to them.

Had the Devil been created out of the fierce <term>womb of nature</term> <note>original: Matrix; in Böhme's philosophy, this refers to the generative, often harsh, foundational layer of reality from which things arise.</note> into Hell, and had he not possessed a divine <term>essence</term> <note>original: Ens; a term used to describe the fundamental spiritual substance or "being" of a creature.</note>, he would feel no pain in Hell. But since he was created in Heaven and yet stirred up the <term>torment</term> <note>original: Qual; Böhme uses this word to imply both "torment" and "source-quality" (Quelle), suggesting that the very source of one's life becomes a pain if misaligned.</note> of darkness within himself, leading himself entirely into that darkness, the light is now a pain to him. It manifests as an eternal despair of God’s grace and a constant enmity; because he cannot endure God within himself and God has spat him out, he is full of spite toward his mother—that is, the eternal nature—from whose essence and being he arose. This mother is the eternal nature, which holds him captive as an apostate in his <term>place</term> <note>original: loco; Latin for "place" or "position."</note> and takes pleasure in him according to the properties of wrath and grimness. Because he refused to help perform God’s <term>joyful play</term> <note>original: frewdenspiel; Böhme often describes the creation and the interaction of divine forces as a "play" or "game" for God's glory.</note>, he must now be the <term>opposite</term> <note>original: contrarium; the opposing force or antithesis.</note> and an enemy of all that is good. For everything comes from God and exists within Him: darkness and light, love and wrath, fire and light. Yet, He calls Himself "God" only according to the light of His love.

There is an eternal <term>contradiction</term> <note>original: Contrarium.</note> between darkness and light: neither <note>The original German "Reines" is likely a transcription error for "Keines," meaning "neither."</note> grasps the other...

<summary>This page explains that human suffering and the Devil's torment arise from turning away from God's love into the "wrathful" side of nature. It establishes Böhme's dualistic view that God contains both Light and Darkness, but is only called "God" in the context of His love.</summary>

...one another, and none is the other, and yet there is but one single Being. But they are distinguished by their <term>source-quality</term> <note>original: Qual; Böhme uses this word to imply both "torment" and the "quality" or "source" of a thing's nature.</note>, as well as by the Will; yet it is not a divisible Being. Only a <term>principle</term> <note>original: principium; a fundamental realm or foundation of existence.</note> separates them, so that one exists within the other as if it were a nothingness—and yet it is there, though not manifest according to the <term>property</term> <note>original: Eygenschafft</note> of the realm in which it resides. For the Devil remained in his dominion, but not in the one in which God created him. Instead, he remained in the anxious birth of eternity, in the <term>Center of Nature</term>, according to the property of <term>wrath</term> <note>original: Grimm; in Böhme's thought, the "wrath" or "fierceness" is the dark, consuming fire of God's nature when separated from His light and love.</note>, which brings forth darkness, anxiety, and torment. He is indeed a prince in the <term>place</term> <note>original: loco; Latin for "place" or "position."</note> of this world, but only in the first <term>principle</term> <note>original: principio</note>: in the kingdom of darkness, in the abyss. He is not a prince in the kingdom of the sun, stars, and elements; in those, he is neither prince nor lord. Rather, he dwells in the portion of wrath, in the very root of the malice of all beings, and yet he does not have the power to act through them.

For in all things there is also a good, which keeps the evil imprisoned and locked within itself. Thus, the Devil can only move and rule within the evil when it is stirred up by an evil desire, leading his own desire into that malice—something that an <term>inanimate creature</term> <note>original: vnlebhaffte Creatur; refers to non-conscious things like minerals or plants that lack a self-determining will.</note> cannot do. But a human being can do it, even through the inanimate creature, if they [direct] the center of their will...

<summary>Böhme explains that the realms of light and darkness coexist within the same "Being" but are separated by their internal principles. He describes the Devil's "dominion" as being restricted to the spiritual "wrath" or "darkness" underlying the physical world, unable to influence the world unless a human will activates that latent evil.</summary>

In the **hailstorm** <term>Schawerschlag</term>, as in the disturbance, he also moves; but he cannot direct it, for he is not the master within it, but a servant.

...it enters into **aversion** <term>Widerwillen</term> <note>original: Widerwillen; literally a "counter-will" or repugnance that turns against the divine order.</note> and into enmity, and therein it is tormented by the aversion; and a false will arises within a good one. For one's own desire always introduces unrest.
