...to God: <note>The catchword from the previous page was "calling" (ruffen), leading into "calling to God."</note> *Abba, dear Father, deliver me from evil* <note>original: Abba lieber Vatter/ erlöse mich vom vbel; a combination of Romans 8:15 and the Lord's Prayer.</note>; then it works—even if it happens that the earthly will becomes too strong in the wrath of **GOD** through the devil’s contagion—only within itself. This is as **St. Paul** also says: *Now if I sin, it is not I who do it, but the sin that dwells in the flesh.* Also, *so now with the mind I serve the law of God, but with the flesh the law of sin.* 

**Paul** does not mean that the mind should consent to the will of the flesh; rather, sin is so strong in the flesh—like the awakened wrath of **GOD** in **selfhood** <term>Selbheit</term> <note>original: Selbheit; the ego-driven state of being focused on one's own self-will rather than God's.</note>—that it is often led into lust by force through a false resonance from **godless people**, or by the sight of worldly vanity <note>original: vppigkeit; refers to extravagance, luxury, or the prideful excess of the world.</note>. This can entirely drown out the resigned will and rule, as if by force. 

And when sin has thus been worked in the flesh, the wrath wants to delight in it and also reaches for the resigned will; then the resigned will cries out to **GOD** for deliverance from the evil—desiring that **GOD** would take the sin away from it and lead it into the **<term>Center</term>** <note>original: Centrum; in Böhme's mystical physics, the 'Center' is the inner core or point of origin where a thing is transformed or brought to its end.</note>, as into death, so that it may die.

And **St. Paul** says further: *There is therefore now no condemnation for those who are in Christ Jesus, who [walk] according to the purpose...* <note>Böhme is quoting Romans 8:1. The sentence breaks off mid-word as the page ends.</note>

<sig>E 2</sig>

...are called; that is, those who are in the purpose <note>original: Vorsatz; this refers to God’s eternal plan or intent for humanity.</note> of **GOD**, in which **GOD** called man, are called again in that same calling: so that they stand once more in the purpose of **GOD**, in which He created man in His likeness, in an image after Himself. As long as the own-will stands in **selfhood** <term>Selbheit</term> <note>original: Selbheit; Böhme’s term for the ego-driven state of being focused on one's own self-will rather than God's.</note>, it is not in the purpose and calling of **GOD**; thus it is not called, for it has gone out of its **place** <term>loco</term> <note>original: loco; Latin for "place." Böhme often speaks of the soul's "proper place" being within the divine will.</note>. 

But when the mind turns itself back again into the calling, as into **resignation** <term>Gelassenheit</term> <note>original: Gelassenheit; a state of yieldedness, surrender, or letting-go of the self-will.</note>, then the will is in the calling of **GOD**, as in the **place** <term>loco</term> where **GOD** created it; then it has the power to become **GOD'S** child, as it is written: *He has given us power to become the children of God.* The power He has given us is His purpose, in which He created man in His image; this power **GOD** has brought back into humanity in **Christ**, and has given that same power a power to crush the head of the sin in the flesh—that is, the serpent’s will and desire <note>An allusion to Genesis 3:15, where the "seed of the woman" is prophesied to crush the head of the serpent.</note>. That is to say: the **resigned will** <term>gelassene wille</term> in **Christ** treads upon the head of the sinful serpent’s will and its desire, and kills the committed sin once more. The given power becomes a death to death, and a power to life for the life.

Therefore no one has any excuse— <note>The catchword "entschuldi-" leads into "entschuldigung" (excuse) on the next page.</note>

No, no; in this soft bed the earthly will only becomes strong, fat, and lustful. It is no hypocrisy, nor are words spoken in vain; we must not be children adopted only from the outside, but children born of GOD from within, in a new man who is resigned <term>gelassen; the state of "Gelassenheit" or yieldedness, where the human will is fully surrendered to the Divine</term> in God.

All the hypocrisy of saying, "Christ has paid and done enough for sin; He died for our sin"—if we do not also die to sin in Him, and put on His merit in a new obedience and live within it—is entirely false and a void, invalid comfort. He alone has the right to comfort himself with Christ's suffering who becomes an enemy to and weary of sin: who does not gladly see, hear, or taste it; who is its enemy; who would always gladly do right and well if he only knew what he should do. # E N D

## Of the Second Book.

<summary>This page concludes the second book of the treatise, transitioning from the internal struggle of the soul to a prophetic celebration of the divine light and organic spiritual growth in Zion. It emphasizes that despite outward silence, the power of God is emerging within the believer.</summary>

## The Third Little Book /
# CONCERNING REBIRTH <note>original Latin: "DE REGENERATIONE"</note>,
### That is:
## On the New Rebirth /

->How a person, who<-
->is earnest about salvation, must allow<-
->himself to be led by Christ’s<-
->Spirit out of the confused and quarrel-<-
->some Babylon <note>In Böhme’s theology, "Babylon" refers to the state of spiritual blindness, worldly vanity, and the verbal strife of institutionalized religion.</note>: So that he may be born<-
->anew in Christ’s Spirit<-
->and live for Him alone, etc.<-

->Authored by<-

## Jacob Böhme /

->Otherwise known as<-

### The Teutonic <term>PHILOSOPHER</term> <note>original: "Teutonicum PHILOSOPHUM." This title refers to Böhme as a German ("Teutonic") mystical philosopher, a name by which he became famous throughout Europe.</note>.

## Revelation 18 <note>original: "Apocal. 18." referring to the Apocalypse or Book of Revelation.</note>.

> Come out of Babylon, my people: so that you do not become partakers of her sins: so that you do not receive any of her plagues. For: her sins reach up to heaven: and **GOD** remembers her wickedness <note>The German "frevel" implies a wanton, defiant outrage or transgression against divine law.</note>.

--- Page 121 ---
<meta>Following the scriptural call to leave the spiritual confusion of "Babylon" on the previous page, Böhme now begins the first chapter of his treatise. He establishes the theological foundation for "Rebirth" by citing the Gospel of John, contrasting the limitations of the mortal, physical body with the necessity of an internal, spiritual transformation.</meta>

->Concerning Rebirth.<-

# Chapter I.

<margin>John 3</margin>
**C**HRIST spoke: Unless you turn back <note>The German "vmbkehret" implies a conversion or a total turning around of the soul's orientation.</note> and become like children, you shall not see the kingdom of God. And again he says to Nicodemus: Unless one is born anew <note>original: "Newgebohren." This is the core concept of spiritual regeneration or "Wiedergeburt."</note> of water and Spirit, he cannot enter into the kingdom of God. For: what is born of the flesh is flesh, and what is born of Spirit is spirit. Scripture clearly testifies that the fleshly, natural man <note>The "natural man" refers to humanity in its fallen, unredeemed state, operating only through reason and physical senses.</note> perceives nothing of the Spirit of God: it is foolishness to him, and he cannot comprehend it.

Since we all now have flesh and blood, and are moreover mortal, as is evident before our eyes, and yet Scripture also says that we are temples of the Holy Spirit who dwells in us; and that the kingdom of **GOD** is within us, and that Christ must take shape <note>original: "gestalt gewinnen." This describes the mystical process of the "New Man" being formed within the believer’s soul.</note> in us, and that he wishes to give us his flesh for food and his blood for drink: and [He] says further, whoever will not eat the flesh of the Son of Man has no life in him. Thus we must indeed consider with all earnestness what kind of man is within us,

<meta>catchword: is/</meta>
<sig>M III</sig>

<summary>In this opening chapter, Böhme uses the Gospel of John to argue that human nature is split between a mortal, "fleshly" side and a hidden, spiritual potential. He asserts that true spiritual life requires a radical "new birth" to allow the Holy Spirit and the "Son of Man" to take shape within the believer.</summary>
<keywords>Rebirth, Nicodemus, Kingdom of God, Flesh and Spirit, Natural Man, Temple of the Holy Spirit, Inner Christ, Regeneration</keywords>

be, who is similar to and capable of [containing] the Godhead.

For of the mortal flesh, which turns to earth and lives in the vanity <note>In Böhme’s time, "vanity" (Eitelkeit) referred to the fleeting, transitory, and empty nature of the material world.</note> of this world, and continually lusts against God, it cannot be said that it is the temple of the Holy Spirit. Much less can it be said that the new rebirth happens in this earthly flesh, since it dies and decays, and is a constant house of sin.

But if it nevertheless remains true that a true Christian is born from Christ, and that the New Rebirth is a temple of the Holy Spirit who dwells in us; and that only the new man born from Christ partakes of the flesh and blood of Christ: then being a Christian is not such a simple <note>original: "schlecht." In 17th-century German, this meant "simple," "plain," or "lowly" rather than "bad." Böhme is arguing that Christianity is a profound ontological change, not a minor lifestyle adjustment.</note> thing. And Christianity does not consist merely in the *history* <note>Böhme uses "Historia" to describe "historical faith"—an intellectual belief in the facts of the Bible that lacks a corresponding inner spiritual transformation.</note>, that we only know it and appropriate that knowledge to ourselves; that we only say, "Christ died for us, and has broken death within us, and turned it into life; He has paid the debt for us; we need only comfort ourselves with this and firmly believe that it has happened."

For: we find within ourselves that sin in the flesh is alive, desirous, and active—that it is at work. Thus the new rebirth from Christ must [be] something else

<meta>catchword: be/</meta>

<summary>Böhme argues that the physical body is too corrupt to be God’s temple, asserting that true "Rebirth" is a profound internal transformation rather than a mere intellectual belief in biblical history.</summary>
<keywords>Godhead, vanity, Holy Spirit, rebirth, historical faith, internal transformation, sin, flesh and blood of Christ</keywords>

<meta>Continuing from the previous page, Böhme is distinguishing between the physical, sinful flesh and the "new man" of the rebirth. He argues that the new spiritual nature—the part of a person truly "born again"—is separate from the mortal body that remains prone to sin.</meta>

# ## ### Von der Wiedergeburt.
->On Rebirth.<-

be—one that does not cooperate with the sinful flesh, and which does not desire sin.

For St. Paul says: That in those who are in Christ Jesus, there is nothing damnable. And further: Should we, who are Christians, still be sinners? Far be it, since we have died to sin in Christ. Also, the man of sin is not a temple of the Holy Spirit. And yet, there is no man who does not sin. God has enclosed all under sin. For the <term>Scripture</term> <note>original: "Schrifft." Böhme uses this term to refer to the authoritative text of the Bible.</note> says: Before you no living person is righteous, if you choose to count their sins. The righteous man falls seven times a day. And yet, this must not be understood to mean that the *righteous* part of him falls and sins, but rather the mortal and sinful part.

For: A Christian's righteousness in Christ cannot sin. For St. Paul says: Our citizenship <note>original: "wandel." In 17th-century German Bibles (Philippians 3:20), this word implies one's conduct, way of life, or place of residence/citizenship.</note> is in heaven, from whence we await the Savior Jesus Christ. Now, if our citizenship is in heaven, then heaven must be within us. Christ dwells in heaven; if we are now His temples, then that same heaven must be within us. Yet, since sin nonetheless assails <note>original: "anficht." This refers to *Anfechtung*, a central Lutheran concept describing spiritual trials, doubts, or the internal "assault" of sin and the devil.</note> us—through which the devil has access to us and within us—so too must hell be within us. For the devil dwells in hell; and wherever he is, there

<summary>Böhme explains the dual nature of the believer, arguing that while the mortal body remains sinful, the "new man" born of Christ is a heavenly temple. He introduces the idea that both heaven and hell exist as internal states within the human soul.</summary>

### Von der Wiedergeburt.

he is in hell and cannot come out of it. And even if he were to possess a person, he still dwells within the person, in hell, as if in God's wrath.

Now we must rightly consider man—what and how he is. And we must see that a true Christian is not merely a <term>historical</term> <note>original: "historischer." Böhme uses this to describe someone who has an intellectual or book-knowledge of the Bible but lacks an internal spiritual experience.</note> new man, as if it were enough for us to profess Christ and believe that he is God’s Son and has paid the price for us. For it is not a matter of an externally <term>imputed righteousness</term> <note>original: "zugerechnete Gerechtigkeit." This refers to the theological concept where God "counts" a believer as righteous because of Christ, rather than the believer actually becoming righteous in their nature. Böhme argues for the latter.</note>—where we merely believe it has happened—but rather an **inborn**, childlike righteousness. Just as the flesh must die, so too must the life and the will die to sin, and must become like a child who knows nothing, but only sighs for the mother who bore it.

Thus, the will of a Christian must enter back into the mother—that is, into the **Spirit of Christ**—and must become a child in its <term>selfhood</term> <note>original: "Selbheit." A central term for Böhme, referring to the ego-driven, self-centered nature of the fallen human will.</note>, its self-willing, and its own power. There, where the will and desire are directed only toward the mother, a new will and obedience in righteousness must arise out of death, from the Spirit of Christ—a will that no longer desires sin.

For the will that allows **vanity** into itself and desires it is not **born again**. And yet, although in the born-again...

<summary>Böhme argues that true Christianity requires an internal rebirth ("inborn righteousness") rather than a mere intellectual belief in the historical Christ or the legal imputation of grace. He uses the metaphor of a child returning to its mother to describe the soul surrendering its "self-will" to the Spirit of Christ.</summary>

...a will remains, which longs for <term>vanity</term> <note>original: "Eitelkeit." In Böhme’s time, this specifically referred to the "emptiness" or "transience" of the material world, which distracts from the eternal.</note> and sins. Thus, we must rightly consider the image of man to see how the new rebirth occurs. For it does not happen in the mortal flesh, and yet it happens truly within us, in flesh and blood, in water and spirit, as the Scripture says.

So we must rightly consider what kind of man is within us who is a limb of Christ and a temple of **GOD**, who dwells in heaven. And then, what kind of man that is whom the devil governs and drives. He cannot drive the temple of Christ, so he is also not much concerned with the mortal flesh. And yet there are not three men within one another, but only a single one.

If we now wish to consider this, we must consider time and eternity, how these are within one another, as well as light and darkness, good and evil; but especially the human <term>origin</term> <note>original: "Vrstand." While it looks like the modern word for "understanding," Böhme uses it here to mean the primordial state or the "standing-ground" of a thing's beginning.</note> and source.

### This is now to be considered as follows.

**W**e look upon the external world, with the stars and the four elements, in which man and all creatures live. This is not, nor is it called, God. God indeed dwells,

<summary>Böhme explains that rebirth is an internal spiritual reality that exists alongside our mortal, sinful nature. He introduces the idea that man is a microcosm where time and eternity, light and darkness, and the spirit and the flesh all coexist.</summary>

well therein; but the essence of the external world does not grasp him. We also have an example of this in the four elements, which in their <term>origin</term> <note>original: "urstande." Böhme refers to the primordial state or the source from which things emerge.</note> are only one element, which is neither hot nor cold, neither dry nor wet; yet it divides itself through movement into four properties: namely Fire, Air, Water, and Earth.

Who would believe that fire could give birth to water? Or that the origin of fire could be in water? We would not believe it if we did not see it with our eyes in the <note>original: "Wetterleuchten." This refers to sheet-lightning or heat-lightning—flashes of light on the horizon without audible thunder, often used by Böhme as a metaphor for divine manifestation.</note> sheet-lightning, and also find it within living beings: that the <term>Essential Fire</term> <note>original: "Essentialische Fewer." Latin for "body."</note>, in the blood, and that the blood is its mother, and the fire is the blood's father.

And just as God now dwells in the world and fulfills all things, yet possesses nothing; and as the fire dwells in the water and does not possess it; and as the light dwells in the darkness, yet does not possess the darkness; the day in the night, and the night in the day; time in eternity, and eternity in time: so too is man created. According to his external humanity, he is time and exists in time,

For when the light becomes manifest in the darkness / then the darkness loses its dark-nature / and is not recognized. So also in reverse / if the darkness arises in the light / and gains power: then the light is extinguished with its power.

Such a thing we must also consider within man. The eternal darkness of the soul / is hell / as a <term>torment of anguish</term> <note>original: "Angstqual." Thus understand: the fire in the light / is a **fire of love** <note>original: "Liebefewer"</note> / a desire for gentleness / and rich in joy. And the fire in the darkness / is a **fire of anguish** <note>original: "Angstfewer." In Böhme's thought, this is the harsh, consuming quality of existence without the light of God.</note> / and is painful / hostile / and in its essence repugnant. These are the seven "source-spirits" or qualities that Böhme believes constitute all of nature.</note> leading to the fire / all stand in the great anguish.

## Chapter II.

**HE**re we are now to consider / how man was created. Moses says rightly: God created man in his own image; yes, in the image of God created he him. We understand this from the eternal and temporal birth / from the inner spiritual world / which he breathed into him / into the created image / and then from the inner spiritual world-essence / which is holy.

For just as in the outer world / [there is] a nature and essence: So also in the inner spiritual world / there is a nature and essence / which is spiritual / out of which the outer world was <term>exhaled</term> <note>original: "außgehauchet." This implies that the physical universe is a literal "out-breathing" or manifestation of the divine spiritual realm.</note> / and born out of light and darkness / and created into a beginning and time.

And out of the inner and outer world—

essence was Man / created in a likeness according to the birth / and out of the birth of all beings. The body is an <term>extract</term> <note>original Latin: "limbus." In Böhme's thought, this refers to the fundamental "seed" or essential matter drawn from both the physical and spiritual realms.</note> of the Earth / and also an <term>extract</term> of the Heavenly being. For / the Earth was breathed out / or spoken forth out of the world of Darkness and Light. Out of this, Man was / as if by the <term>Word of creation</term> <note>original Latin: "Verbo fiat," referring to the divine command "Let there be."</note> / as if in eternal desire / contained and created into an image / out of Time and Eternity.

This image was in the inner and spiritual element / out of which the <term>four elements</term> <note>original Latin: "4. Elementa." Böhme believes the physical world of earth, water, air, and fire emerged from a single, pure spiritual "Element."</note> proceed and are born. In that single element / was <term>Paradise</term>; For the properties of nature / out of the world of Fire, Darkness, and Light / were all in equal <term>harmony</term> <note>original Latin: "concordantz."</note> / measure and weight / none was / manifest in particular before the other. Therefore / there was also no fragility within it. For / one property did not overpower the other: There was no strife or conflict / between the powers and properties.

Into this created image / God breathed the spirit and breath of understanding / out of all three worlds / as a single soul: which is in the inner world of Darkness / and Fire of the eternal spiritual nature. According to which God / calls himself a strong / jealous God / and a consuming fire.

<summary>Böhme describes the creation of Man as a composite of both earthly and heavenly "extracts," existing originally in a state of perfect elemental harmony. He identifies the human soul as a breath from God that contains the qualities of all three cosmic worlds, including the "consuming fire" of the divine nature.</summary>

# On Rebirth.

This is now the eternal, great natural soul / a magical fiery breath / in which is the <term>origin</term> <note>original German: "Urstandt." In the theology of Jakob Böhme, this refers to the primordial foundation or the "original standing" of a thing before it is fully manifested.</note> of the fire of life; out of the great power of transformation. In this property is God’s wrath / as well as the eternal Darkness: in so far as the fire gives no light.

The other property of the breath of God is the <term>source-spirit</term> <note>original German: "Qualgeist." This is a technical term used by Böhme to describe the inner pulsing, "qualing," or active tension that produces movement and sensation within a being.</note> of the Light / out of the great fiery desire of love / out of the great gentleness / according to which God / names Himself a dear, merciful **GOD**: In which the true spirit of understanding and life stands in power.

For / just as out of every fire / a light shines: And in the light the power of understanding is recognized. So too is the breath of light attached to the fiery breath of God / and was breathed into the human image.

The third property of the <term>breath of God</term> / was the outer air / with the air-constellation <note>original German: "Lufft-Gestirne." This refers to the astral influences or the "spirit of the world" that governs the physical body and its environment.</note>: wherein was the life of the outer being and the body and the stars. This He breathed into his nostrils. And just as Time and Eternity hang together / and Time is born out of Eternity / So also the inner breath of **GOD** hung / upon the outer / and became to Man this threefold soul / at once breathed in—

<sig>F 3</sig>
<meta>Catchword: breathed—</meta>

<summary>This page explains the "threefold soul" of Man, composed of a fiery natural origin, a gentle light-spirit of understanding, and an external physical/astral breath. It establishes the soul as a bridge between the darkness of wrath, the light of God, and the physical world.</summary>
<keywords>threefold soul, breath of God, source-spirit, fire and light, creation of man, Jakob Böhme, wrath and love</keywords>

--- Page 132 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the creation of man, specifically how the different parts of the human being received their respective spirits. It follows the previous page's discussion of the "threefold soul." The catchword "Then/" likely leads into a description of the Fall or the subsequent change in this state.</meta>

<page-num>126</page-num>

<header>->On Rebirth.<-</header>

...breathed. Each essence of the **body** <note>original: "Corporis." Latin for 'of the body.'</note> took on the spirit according to its own **property** <note>original: "engenschafft." In Böhme's theology, a 'property' is an essential quality or driving force of a being's nature.</note>: Thus the outer flesh took in the outer air with its constellations, for a life of reason and growth, for the manifestation of the wonders of God. And the **light-body** <note>original: "Lichts-Leib." This refers to a spiritual, celestial body that Böhme believed existed within Adam before the Fall, which was made of divine light rather than heavy matter.</note>—or the heavenly essence—took in the breath of light from the great Divine powers: which breath is called the **Holy Spirit**.

Thus the light penetrated the darkness, just as it penetrated the dark fiery-breath, and also the outer air-breath in its constellation: and it took the power away from all properties, so that the anguish of the fiery-breath in the inner property of the soul—as well as the heat and cold, and all other properties of the outer constellation—could not and might not be manifest. The properties of all **three worlds** <note>Böhme envisions three "worlds" or dimensions: the dark/fiery world of the soul, the light world of God's spirit, and the external/physical world.</note> in soul and body stood in equal **harmony** <note>original: "Concordantz."</note> and balance: the inner holy realm ruled through the outer, as if through the outer powers of the outer life, the outer constellation, and the four elements.

And that was the holy **Paradise**. Thus Man stood in heaven and also in the outer world: and was a lord of all creatures of this world. Nothing could have broken him.

The creative force of the spirit that can bridge time and eternity to manifest physical reality.</term> of time and eternity: from which the earth, with its properties, had been breathed out. Thus the **word of command**, as the strongest desire of eternal nature, pulled the **essence** <note>original: "Essenz." The fundamental substance or vital power within physical things.</note> of the earth.

### Restoration of that which Adam had lost.

Thus God reconciled his wrath, in human quality through the sacrifice, within the aim of the Covenant. In which Covenant the most holy, sweet name **JESUS** had incorporated itself out of the holy name and great power **JEHOVAH**, so that He wished to move and manifest Himself again within the heavenly world-being <note>original: "Weltwesen." In the author's mystical view, this refers to the spiritual substance of the divine realm that was lost or "faded" in humanity after Adam's sin.</note>, which had faded away in Adam, and to rekindle the holy Divine life within it.

This aim of the Covenant was propagated from Adam and his children, from human to human: and pressed from one upon all. Just as sin and the awakened vanity also pressed from one upon all. And it stood in the promise of the Covenant, at the end, in the root of David, in Mary the Virgin: Who was, in the inner kingdom of hidden humanity—as the faded essence <term>essence</term> <note>original: "Wesenheit."</note> belonging to God's kingdom—the daughter of God's Covenant; and in the outer, according to natural humanity, conceived by her true physical father Joachim and her true mother Anna, out of their bodily and soulful essences <term>essences</term> <note>original: "Effentien."</note> and beings: Equal to all other children of Adam: a true daughter of **Eve**.

<summary>This page describes how the name of Jesus emerged from Jehovah to restore the divine life lost by Adam, tracing the path of this promise through history to the Virgin Mary.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the theological argument from page 136 regarding the restoration of humanity. While the previous page traced the promise of the Covenant through history to the Virgin Mary, this page explains the mystical mechanism of the Incarnation—how the divine Word actually entered human nature to revive the spiritual essence lost by Adam.</meta>

<page-num>137</page-num>
-># On Rebirth<-

In this Virgin Mary—at the promised goal of the Covenant of which all the prophets prophesied—the Ever-speaking Word, which created all things, moved itself in the name **JESUS** in the fullness of time, according to its highest and deepest love and humility. And it introduced living, Divine, Heavenly essence into that part of humanity which had faded in Adam—the heavenly part that died within him in Paradise—into the seed of Mary. Understand this [as occurring] in the <term>tincture</term> <note>In the mystical and alchemical tradition of this author, a "tincture" is a spiritual or life-giving force that penetrates and transforms matter.</note> of love, as into the very quality through which Adam was meant to propagate himself in a magical, heavenly way: the true seed of the woman of heavenly essence which had faded in Paradise.

When the Divine light was extinguished in that same heavenly <term>essence</term> <note>original: "Essentz." Referring to the fundamental spiritual substance of a being.</note>, God’s Word—as the divine power of divine understanding—introduced heavenly living essence and awakened the faded essence in the seed of Mary, birthing it into life.

And so, God’s being (in which God dwells and works) and man’s faded being have now become one Person.

For the holy Divine essence anointed the faded one: therefore the Person is called **CHRIST**, an Anointed One of God.

Refers to the physical laws and social order of the material world, often seen by mystics as a distraction from the spiritual realm.</note>. And therein the birth-of-sin is revealed.

But the <term>Fire-breath</term> of the inner world, once it is enlightened, understands it; it has its great sighing, lamenting, hungering, and thirsting for the **sweet Fountain of Christ** <note>original: "süssen Brünlein Christi." It refreshes itself through hungering and desiring—which is true **faith**—in the sweet Fountain of Christ, drawing from its new body of the <term>Heavenly Essence</term>, like a hungry vine on the **vine-stock of Christ**.

And that is the reason why the fiery soul cannot come to perfection during this time: because it stands bound to the outer bond of vanity. Through this bond, the **Devil** constantly shoots his poisonous rays at the soul and sifts it <note>A reference to Luke 22:31, where Satan asks to "sift" the disciples like wheat.</note>, so that the soul sometimes bites <note>Likening the soul to a fish biting a poisoned hook.</note> and poisons itself.

From this, misery and anxiety arise, because the noble **Sophia** <note>In mystical tradition, Sophia represents Divine Wisdom, often personified as a feminine principle or the "Bride" of the soul.</note> hides herself in the **little fountain of Christ** within the heavenly humanity and does not wish to approach vanity.

For she knows how it went for her in **Adam**: when she lost her **pearl** <note>A common metaphor for the spark of divine grace or the kingdom of heaven within the soul.</note>, which is given back to the inner humanity through grace. Beauty, power, and glory are your best possessions; in them you can have joy. Why would you lead yourself into anxiety, distress, and mockery? Strive for the pleasure that does good to the flesh and the mind.

With such filth, a true human being is then often defiled. Just as the outer man defiles himself, like a sow in the mud, and darkens his **noble image**. # On Rebirth.
## Chapter V.

**H**ere a Christian should now consider why he calls himself a Christian, and well observe whether he truly is one. For, merely learning to know and understand that I am a sinner, and that Christ has killed my sin on the **Cross** and shed His blood for me, does not yet by any means make a Christian out of me. The inheritance belongs to the children alone. A maid in the house knows well what the mistress likes, but that does not therefore make her an heir to the mistress's goods. The devil also knows that there is a God; that does not therefore make him an angel again. But if the maid in the house marries the mistress’s son, then she may well come into the inheritance of the mistress's goods.

The same is to be understood in our Christianity. The **children of history** <note>original: "Historien Kinder." This refers to people who have an intellectual or "historical" knowledge of the Bible and Christian doctrine but have not experienced an internal, spiritual transformation.</note> are not heirs to the goods of Christ; rather, the legitimate children are those who are born anew from Christ’s Spirit. For, **God** said to **Abraham**:
> Cast out the son of the bondwoman; he shall not inherit with the free woman. <note>A reference to Genesis 21:10 and Galatians 4:30, often used in mystical theology to distinguish between the "fleshly" law and "spiritual" grace.</note>

For he was a mocker, and only a "history-son" of the faith and spirit of **Abraham**. And as long as he was such a person, <note>The text cuts off here, continuing the thought on the following page regarding the status of the "fleshly" man.</note>

<summary>This page defines true Christianity not as intellectual knowledge of doctrine ("historical children"), but as a spiritual rebirth and union with Christ. It uses the biblical allegory of Sarah and Hagar to distinguish between those who merely know the Word and those who are transformed by it.</summary>

He [must] bring it into effect <note>original: "ins werck richten." To put faith into practice or manifest it through actions.</note> / not just will [it] / and not do [it]. In the willing stands the drawing of the Father to **Christ** <note>A reference to John 6:44: "No one can come to me unless the Father who sent me draws them."</note> / but in the doing stands the true **Life**.

The unregenerate, purely physical or "soulish" nature of man that remains driven by earthly instincts rather than the Divine Spirit.</note> in vanity. 

For / if someone says / I have the will / and would gladly do good / but I have earthly flesh that holds me / so that I cannot / yet I shall be saved by **Grace** for the sake of **Christ's** merit. For / I certainly comfort myself with his suffering and merit / he will receive me by grace without all my merit / and forgive my sin. He acts like one / who knew of a good food for his health / and did not eat it / but ate instead a poisonous one / from which he would become sick and die. 

What does it help the soul / that it knows the Way to good / and will not walk it / but goes the wrong way / and does not reach **God**. What does it help the soul / that it [boasts] of the **sonship** <note>original: "Kindschafft." The state of being a child of God or an heir to Christ.</note> of **Christ** / of his suffering and—

This comforting belongs to the **repentant sinner** / who is in the struggle against sin and God's wrath / when the **trials** <note>original: "Anfechtungen." A term for intense spiritual or psychological struggles where one feels abandoned by God or besieged by the Devil.</note> come / and the Devil besets the soul. **Christ** has indeed earned it alone / but he did not earn it as a [mere] merit (For a reward is given out of merit) / as if he should give us **Childship** from the outside out of his merit / and thus receive us into **Childship**. No / he is the merit himself / He is the open gate through death: Through which we must enter. He does not, however, receive **animals** <note>original: "Thier." Here referring to the "animalistic man" who remains driven by worldly instincts rather than the Spirit.</note> into his merit: But only those / who turn back / and become like children.

These same children who come to him / are his earned reward. For / he also spoke thus: Father / the people were yours / and <note>A reference to John 17:6: "They were yours; you gave them to me and they have obeyed your word."</note>

This same inborn essence is not of this world / but in **Heaven** / of which **St. Paul** says: *Our conduct is in heaven* <note>original: "Vnser Wandel ist im Himmel." A reference to Philippians 3:20. The word "Wandel" refers to one's citizenship, way of life, or social commerce.</note> / the child-like essence walks in **Heaven** / and **Heaven** is in the human being. If, however, **Heaven** is not open within the human / and he only stands before **Heaven** acting hypocritically / and says: "I am still outside / but **Christ** will receive me out of **Grace** / his merit is indeed mine" — Such a person is, according to the external man, in **vanity** and **sins** / and with the soul in **Hell** / as if in **God's wrath**.

Therefore / learn to understand it rightly / what **Christ** has taught us and done. He is our **Heaven** / he must [take] a **form** in us...

Sin is only rebuked and made
manifest.

In this mystical theology, this refers to the corrupted, ego-driven nature humans inherited from the Fall of Adam, influenced by the serpent.</note>. When the sun rises / then the night is swallowed up in the day / and there is no more night. In this way the forgiveness of sins occurs. The Spirit of Christ eats from His <term>holy being</term> <note>original: "H. wesen." Or what does it concern the devils / or the soul in God's wrath? They eat from their own heavenly body / in which heaven they dwell / as if in the abyss.

Likewise also in the <term>preaching office</term> <note>original: "Predigampte." The formal role of the minister in delivering a sermon.</note> / the godless man hears what the external soul preaches to the external world / he accepts that / as a <term>historical account</term> <note>original: "Historiam" (Latin). A literal or intellectual understanding of the Bible as a record of past events, lacking personal spiritual experience.</note>. But if there is perhaps stubble or straw in the preaching / he sucks vanity from it / and the soul sucks therefrom the false poison / and murders of the devil. With this she tickles herself / hearing / how she can judge men. But if the preacher is also a dead man / and sows poison and reproach out of his passions / then the devil teaches / and the devil hears.

->## On Rebirth.<-

The same teaching is captured in the godless heart and brings forth godless fruits, out of which the world has become a <term>murderer’s den</term> <note>original: "Mordgrube." A biblical allusion (Matthew 21:13) suggesting the spiritual world has been corrupted into a place of predatory behavior.</note> of the devil; so that from both the teacher and the listener, nothing is inside but vain mocking, blaspheming, scorning, word-wrangling, and biting at one another for advantage.

But in the holy teacher, the Holy Spirit teaches; and in the holy listener, the Spirit of Christ hears through the soul and the divine housing of the **divine resonance** <note>original: "Göttlichen Schalles." The inner "sound" or "echo" of God's Word vibrating within the believer's soul.</note>. The holy person has his church within himself, wherein he hears and teaches; but <term>Babel</term> <note>original: "Babel." In Böhme’s mystical vocabulary, this represents the "Church of Confusion"—the institutionalized, external religious systems that lack true spiritual life.</note> has a <term>stone-heap</term> <note>original: "Steinhauffen." A derogatory term for physical church buildings when they are treated as the only location of God's presence, ignoring the "temple" of the heart.</note>, into which she enters to practice hypocrisy and outward show; she lets herself be seen in beautiful clothes, pretending to be devout and pious. The stone church is her god, in which she places her trust. 

But the holy person has his church with him and in him at all places. For whether he stands or walks, lies or sits, he is in his church; he is in the true Christian church, in the **temple of Christ**. The Holy Spirit preaches to him from all creatures. Everything he looks upon, there he sees a preacher of God.

Here the mocker will say: "I despise the stone church where the congregation gathers." To that I say: No. Rather, I point to the hypocritical **Babylonian Whore**, who with the stone church...

...only practices fornication and calls himself a Christian, but is a <term>whore’s brat</term> <note>original: "Hurenbalck." A derogatory term for a bastard; here used spiritually to describe someone who claims God as a Father but lives a life of sin.</note>.

If I were to go to church for 1,000 years, and also take the Sacrament every week, and even let myself be absolved every day—if I do not have Christ within me, then it is all false and a useless trifle, a <term>carved-image</term> <note>original: "Schnitzwerck." The holy person does holy works out of the holy power of his mind. The work is not the reconciliation, but it is the building that the true Spirit builds in its essence. It is his dwelling house. Just as for the false Christian his fables are his dwelling house, where his soul then goes to practice hypocrisy. Outward hearing goes into the outward and works in the outward; and inward hearing goes into the inward and works in the inward.

The "Spiritus Mundi," the spirit that governs the physical, temporary world.</note> makes a model or carving according to the inner. ## Chapter VII.

A true person who is <term>newly born</term> <note>original: "Newgebohren." The central theme of the book: a spiritual transformation where the soul is reborn into the light of Christ.</note> in Christ’s Spirit lives in the simplicity of Christ and has no quarrel with anyone over religion. He has enough conflict within himself against his animal-like, evil flesh and blood. He always considers himself a great sinner and fears God. But the love of Christ breaks through and drives [the fear] away, just as the day swallows the night.

But for the godless, his sins rest in the sleep of death and turn green <note>Meaning they grow or flourish like weeds.</note> in the <term>abyss</term> <note>original: "Abgrunde." The dark foundation of existence, or the "dark world," where the soul exists when separated from God's light.</note> and bring forth fruit in hell.

Christendom in <term>Babel</term> <note>Böhme’s term for the confused, institutionalized, and sectarian churches of his day, named after the Tower of Babel.</note> quarrels over <term>knowledge</term> <note>original: "Wissenschafft." In this period, the term referred to systematic theological doctrines and academic learning rather than modern physical science.</note>, about how one should serve, honor, and know God; what He is according to His essence and will. And they teach poorly, [claiming] that whoever does not agree with them in all matters of knowledge and opinion is... <note>The page ends mid-sentence; the catchword "kein" (no/not) suggests the next page continues with "not a Christian."</note>

<summary>Böhme explains that a true Christian avoids religious arguments and focuses on internal spiritual struggle, while the institutional church ("Babel") wastes time quarreling over intellectual doctrines.</summary>

<page-num>160</page-num>
<header>On Rebirth.</header>

...[is] no Christian, but a heretic. Now I would indeed like to see how one might bring all their <term>sects</term> <note>original: "Secten." Referring to the various warring religious denominations of the 17th century, such as Lutheranism, Calvinism, and Catholicism.</note> together into one that could call itself the Christian Church, since they are all together only despisers, where each group slanders the other and cries out that they are false.

A Christian, however, has no sects; he can live in the midst of sects, even appear in their worship, and yet he belongs to no sect. He has only a single <term>knowledge</term> <note>original: "Wissenschafft." In Böhme’s time, this meant a systematic understanding or a body of wisdom, rather than modern laboratory science.</note>, which is Christ in him. He seeks only one way, which is the <term>desire</term> <note>original: "Begierde." A central concept in Böhme's writing, referring to the soul's intense spiritual hunger or longing that "draws" God's essence into itself.</note>: that he would always gladly do and teach right, and he places all his knowledge and will into the life of Christ. He sighs and wishes always that God's will might happen in him, and His kingdom be revealed in him. He kills sin in the flesh daily and hourly. 

Then: the <term>woman’s seed</term> <note>original: "deß Weibes Samen." A reference to Genesis 3:15. Böhme interprets this "seed" as the "inner man" or the new spiritual life that defeats the "serpent" of the fallen nature.</note>, as the inner man in Christ, constantly treads upon the head of the Devil in vanity. His faith is a desire for **GOD**, which he has wrapped in certain hope: therefore he ventures it upon the words of the promise. He lives and dies therein. And yet, according to the true man, he never dies. 

For, Christ also says this: *Whoever believes in me will never die: but he has passed through from death to life.* <note>A paraphrase of John 11:26 and John 5:24.</note>

<summary>Böhme argues that a true Christian remains unified in Christ regardless of outward denominational "sects" and describes the internal process of the "inner man" overcoming sin and death.</summary>

# On Rebirth.

...pressed through. Likewise, streams of living water shall flow from him, in the form of good teaching and works.

Therefore I say, all is Babel <note>Böhme uses "Babel" (referring to the Tower of Babel) to symbolize the confusion, pride, and linguistic discord of the organized churches of his day, which he believed had lost the true spirit of Christianity.</note> which bites at one another and quarrels over the letters of the word. The letters all reside in one single root: which is the Spirit of God. Just as the various flowers all stand in the earth and all grow alongside one another. None bites the other over their colors, scent, or taste. They allow the earth and sun, as well as rain and wind, heat and cold, to do with them as they will; but they each grow in their own <term>essence</term> <note>original: "Essentz." In Böhme's thought, this is the spiritual substance or "inner being" that forms the basis of all physical things.</note> and <term>quality</term> <note>original: "Eygenschafft." The specific natural characteristics or innate properties that define a person's or thing's unique role in creation.</note>.

Thus it is also with the children of God: they have various gifts and <term>knowledge</term> <note>original: "Erkäntnuß." This refers to spiritual insight or experiential wisdom rather than mere book-learning.</note>, but all from one Spirit. They rejoice alongside one another in the great wonders of God and thank the Most High in His wisdom. Why should they quarrel long over Him in whom they live and are, and whose very being they themselves are?

It is the greatest folly in Babel that the Devil has made the world quarrel over religion, so that they quarrel over self-made opinions. They quarrel over the letters; yet the Kingdom of God resides in no opinion, but rather in <term>power</term> <note>original: "Krafft." A central concept for Böhme, meaning the active, creative, and divine energy that flows from God into the soul.</note> and in love. Also Christ said, and left it to His disciples at the last: that they should **love** <note>The catchword from the bottom of the page "lieben/" completes the sentence: "...should love one another."</note> one another.

<summary>Böhme argues that sectarian conflict over religious dogma is a sign of spiritual confusion, comparing the diversity of true believers to flowers in a garden that grow together in harmony despite their differences. He emphasizes that the Kingdom of God is found in divine power and love rather than in intellectual opinions or the literal "letter" of the law.</summary>

...love; by this everyone would recognize that they were His disciples, just as He had loved them. If people strove for love and righteousness as much as they do for opinion <note>original: "meynung." In this context, it refers to personal dogmas, sectarian assertions, or intellectual stances that cause division.</note>, there would be no strife on Earth at all: we would live as children in our Father and would need no law nor order.

We all have only one single <term>order</term> <note>original: "Orden." Here meaning a religious rule or organized community structure.</note>, which is to keep still before the Lord of all beings, and surrender our will to Him, and let His Spirit work, play, and do in us what He will; and whatever He works and reveals in us, we offer back to Him as His fruit.

...lived as children of one mother, just as the branches on a tree all draw sap <note>original: "Safft." Used here to denote the vital spiritual life-force.</note> from a single root: then we would be much holier.

Knowledge exists only for this purpose: that we might learn it because we have lost the **Divine Power** <note>original: "Göttliche Krafft." This refers to the primordial energy and image of God that Böhme believes was lost during the Fall.</note> in **Adam**, and are now inclined toward evil; so that we may learn to recognize how we possess evil qualities within us, and that doing evil does not please God: so that through knowledge we might learn to act rightly. But if we possess the **Power of God** within us, and desire with all our strength to act rightly and live rightly: then knowledge is merely our **play** <note>original: "Spiel." In Böhme’s theology, "play" describes the joyful, unforced, and creative manifestation of God's wisdom through human beings.</note>, in which we rejoice.

For true <term>knowledge</term> <note>original: "Wissen." Here contrasted with "Erkäntnuß" (insight), implying that when one is reborn, formal learning becomes a secondary delight rather than a necessary rule.</note> is the revelation of the **Spirit of God** through eternal **Wisdom**: He knows what He wills in His children. He pours out His wisdom and wonders through His children, just as the earth pours out manifold flowers. If we now lived alongside one another in the **Spirit of Christ** as humble children, and each rejoiced in the other’s gifts and <term>insight</term> <note>original: "Erkäntnuß."</note>, who would want to judge us?

Who judges the birds in the forest, who praise the Lord of all beings with manifold voices, each in its own **essence** <note>original: "Essenz." This refers to the inner nature or fundamental being of a creature as it was created by God.</note>? Therefore, those people who wrangle over <term>knowledge</term> <note>original: "Wiſſenſchafft." In Böhme's 17th-century context, this refers to systematic doctrinal learning or intellectual expertise rather than modern laboratory science.</note> and for God's sake, and despise one another because of it, are more foolish than the birds in the woods and the wild animals, which have no proper understanding. They are, before **Holy God**, more useless than the meadow flowers, which yet keep still for the **Spirit of God** and allow **Divine Wisdom** and **Power** to reveal themselves through them. Indeed, they are worse than the thistles and thorns among the beautiful flowers, which at least stand still. They are like the predatory animals and birds in the forest, which frighten the other birds away from their singing and the praise of God.

In short: They are the devil's growth in the **wrath of God** <note>original: "Zorne Gottes." In Böhme’s theology, the "wrath" represents the harsh, dark, and consuming fire of the first principle of nature when it is not permeated by the light of Divine Love.</note>, who through their torment must nonetheless serve the Lord. ## Chapter VIII.

**T**he entire Christian religion consists in this: that we learn to recognize ourselves—who we are, where we have come from, and how we passed out of <term>unity</term> <note>original: "einigung." In Böhme's thought, this represents the primordial state of being at one with God's will.</note> into <term>disunity</term> <note>original: "Vneinigkeit." This refers to the fragmented, conflicted state of humanity after the Fall.</note>, wickedness, and injustice, and how we have awakened these things within ourselves. Secondly: where we were when we were in unity, when we were children of God. Thirdly: how we are now in disunity, in conflict and opposition. Fourthly: where we are journeying to from this fragile existence—where we are headed with the immortal part, and also with the mortal.

In these four points, our entire religion is contained: to learn how to emerge from disunity and <term>vanity</term> <note>original: "Eitelkeit." In this context, it refers to the empty, fleeting, and illusory nature of the material world separated from God.</note>, and to enter again into the one tree from which we all came in <term>Adam</term> <note>Böhme views Adam not just as a historical figure, but as the original "Universal Man" whose fall fragmented human nature.</note>—which is **Christ in us**. We have no need to dispute about anything, nor do we have any conflict. Let each person only learn to practice how they may enter once more into the love of God and of their brother.

Christ's <term>Testaments</term> <note>original: "Testamenta." This refers to the Sacraments, specifically Baptism and the Lord's Supper, which Böhme viewed as essential means of reconnecting with the divine essence.</note> are nothing other than a brotherly cove- <meta>Catchword: nuß (covenant)</meta>

<summary>This page defines the core of the Christian life as a four-fold process of self-knowledge: understanding our origin in divine unity, our current state of fallen conflict, and our eventual eternal destination. Böhme emphasizes that true religion is found in returning to the "tree" of Christ rather than in external theological disputes.</summary>

## On Rebirth.

...knowledge <note>The text continues from the previous page: "...learn to practice how they may enter once more into the [knowledge]..."</note> that God binds Himself with us in Christ, and we in Him. All teaching should aim for this, as well as all willing, living, and doing. Whatever teaches or acts otherwise is <term>Babel</term> <note>In Böhme’s writing, "Babel" refers to the "Tower of Confusion"—specifically the organized, academic, and warring church denominations of his time that lacked true spiritual life.</note> and a fable, merely a carving of pride for useless judgment, and a confusing of the world: a hypocrisy of the Devil with which he blinds simplicity.

Everything that teaches outside of God's Spirit, and does not possess Divine Knowledge, yet sets itself up as a teacher in God's Kingdom and wishes to serve God with teaching—that is false, and serves only its own <term>belly-god</term> <note>original: "Abgott-bauche." A strong 17th-century metaphor for teachers who seek only their own comfort, status, and physical appetites rather than spiritual truth.</note> and its proud, haughty mind, desiring to be honored and called holy. Such a person carries an office chosen by the children of men, who have appointed them only out of flattery and for the sake of favor. Christ said: Whoever does not enter the sheepfold through the door—that is, through Him—but climbs in another way, is a thief and a murderer; and the sheep do not follow him. <note>Referencing John 10:1.</note>

For they do not recognize his voice. He does not have the voice of the Spirit of **GOD**, but only the voice of his own art and his own learning. He teaches, and not God’s Spirit. But Christ says: Every plant which my Heavenly Father has not planted shall be uprooted. <note>Referencing Matthew 15:13.</note>

<summary>Böhme argues that true Christian teaching must be rooted in the union with Christ through the Holy Spirit; anything else is mere "Babel" or human pride. He warns against false teachers who rely on intellectual "art" or human appointment rather than divine inspiration.</summary>

## On Rebirth.

How then can the godless plant heavenly plants? Since he has no seed of power within himself? Christ says plainly: The sheep do not hear his voice; they do not follow him. <note>Referencing John 10:5.</note>

The written word is only a <term>tool</term> <note>original: "Werckzeug." In Böhme's theology, the external word (scripture) is the instrument, but the inner spirit is the true agent.</note> with which the Spirit leads. The word that wishes to teach must be alive within the literal word. The Spirit of God must resonate within the literal sound; otherwise, no one is a teacher of God, but only a teacher of letters, a knower of histories, and not of the Spirit of God in Christ. Everything with which one wishes to serve God must happen in faith, that is, in the Spirit; this makes the work complete and pleasing to **GOD**. What a person begins and does in faith, he does in the Spirit of God, who works together in the deed. This is pleasing to **GOD**; for He has made it Himself, and His power is within it; it is holy.

But whatever is done in <term>Selfhood</term> <note>original: "Selbheit." This refers to the ego-driven state of fallen man, acting for himself rather than through God's will.</note> without faith, that is only a figure or a husk of a true Christian work.

If you serve your brother and do it only out of <term>stinginess</term> <note>original: "g'eißnerey." An archaic form of "Geiz" (greed or avarice), implying a begrudging or miserly spirit.</note> and give to him unwillingly, then you do not serve **GOD**. For your faith does not proceed out of love, into the hope <meta>catchword: in</meta>

<summary>Böhme argues that true spiritual teaching and service must come from the Spirit of God within the person, rather than from academic learning or outward "Selfhood." He emphasizes that works done without faith and love are merely empty husks.</summary>

into your gift. You may well serve your brother, and he for his part thanks God and also blesses you for his part. But you do not bless him: for you give him a grumbling spirit in your gift, which does not enter into God’s Spirit, into the hope of faith. It is also to be understood the same way with receiving: if one gives in faith in divine hope, he blesses his gift in his faith. But he who receives it ungratefully and grumbles in spirit, he curses it in the <term>partaking</term> <note>original: "niessung." But in the godless, who are not capable of this, the wrath of God is stirred. If one sows strife, contempt, and misinterpretation, all godless people take that in; it also takes root and bears such fruit that people mock, deride, slander, and misinterpret one another. ### Of the
# Supersensual Life /
## Being a Dialogue between a Master and a Disciple.

**D**er <note>The decorative initial 'D' marks the beginning of the discourse.</note> Disciple spoke to the Master: How may I come to the <term>supersensual</term> <note>original: "vber-Sinnlichen." In Böhme's terminology, this refers to a state of being "above" or "beyond" the reach of the five physical senses; a spiritual realm of direct perception.</note> life, so that I may see God and hear Him speak?

The Master spoke: When you can swing yourself for a moment into that place where no <term>creature</term> <note>original: "Creatur." In this context, it refers to any created, finite thing or being that exists within time and space, as opposed to the uncreated Creator.</note> dwells, then you hear what God speaks.

The Disciple spoke: Is that near or far?

The Master spoke: It is in you; and if you can be silent for one hour from all your willing and sensing, then you shall hear the unspeakable words of God.

The Disciple spoke: How may I hear, if I stand still from sensing and willing?

The Master spoke: When you stand still from the sensing and willing of your <term>selfhood</term> <note>original: "Selbheit." A central concept in Böhme’s mysticism, referring to the ego or the self-centered nature that seeks its own will rather than God's will.</note>, then the eternal hearing, seeing, and speaking will become manifest in you, and God hears and sees through you. Your own hearing, willing, and seeing hinders you, so that you do not see nor hear **GOD**.

The Disciple spoke: With what shall I hear and see God, since He is above nature and creature?

<summary>This page opens the famous dialogue between a Master and a Disciple, outlining the fundamental mystical requirement of silencing the individual will and the physical senses to perceive the divine.</summary>
<keywords>supersensual life, Jacob Böhme, selfhood, silence, spiritual perception, divine hearing, creature vs creator</keywords>

The Master spoke: When you are <term>silent</term> <note>original: "stille-schweigest." In Böhme's context, this is not just the absence of speech, but the silencing of the ego's internal desires and intellectual striving.</note>, then you are that which God was before <term>nature</term> and <term>creature</term> <note>original: "Natur vnd Creatur." Nature refers to the manifested physical and spiritual world; the creature refers to individual beings or finite things created by God.</note>, out of which He made your nature and creature: So you hear and see it with that with which God saw and heard in you, before your own willing, seeing, and hearing began.

The Disciple spoke: What holds me back then, so that I may not come to that place?

The Master spoke: Your own willing, hearing, and seeing; and that you strive against that out of which you have come. With your own willing, you break yourself off from God’s will, and with your own seeing, you see only into your own willing. And your willing stops up your hearing with a <term>narrow-mindedness</term> <note>original: "Engen-sintigkeit." This denotes a state where the mind is constricted or "narrowed" by its focus on earthly, material things, preventing spiritual expansion.</note> toward earthly, natural things, and leads you into a <term>ground</term> <note>original: "grund." A fundamental concept in German mysticism referring to the deepest essence or foundation of a being's soul.</note>, and overshadows you with that which you will, so that you may not come to the supernatural, supersensual state.

The Disciple spoke: Since I stand in nature, how may I come through nature into the supersensual ground without the <term>destruction</term> <note>original: "zerbrechung." Literally a "breaking" or "shattering." The disciple is concerned that spiritual enlightenment requires the literal death or destruction of his physical, natural self.</note> of nature?

The Master spoke: To that end, three things are required. The first is, that you surrender your will to God, and sink yourself to the very ground in His mercy. The second is, that you hate your own will—

<summary>The Master explains that the Disciple’s own ego and self-will are the primary obstacles to spiritual perception, and begins outlining the three-step process for reaching the supersensual state through surrender.</summary>

...and do not do what your will drives you to: The third is that you submit yourself to the ✠ <note>The cross symbol represents the "Cross of Resignation." **The Disciple said:** But what would happen to him if **God’s** wrath <note>In Böhme’s theology, the "wrath of God" (Zorn Gottes) is not just an emotion, but the "dark fire" or the harsh, consuming foundation of nature that must be overcome by Love.</note> attacked him from within, and the evil world from without, just as happened to our **Lord Christ**?

**The Disciple said:** But how would his body be nourished in the world, and how would he provide for his own, if the disfavor of the whole world should fall upon him?

**The Master said:** He receives a greater favor than the world is capable of giving; for he has **God** and all His angels as friends, who protect him in every need.

...those who did not belong to him and were not his members and brothers; yet those loved him who were capable of <note>original: "seiner Worte fähig waren." ...ch is best—you do not love yourself as a **thine-hood** <term>Deinheit: A term coined by Böhme to describe the possessive, self-centered nature of the human ego that treats its life as its own property rather than a gift from God.</term>, but as a given love of **God**: you love the **Divine ground** <note>original: "Göttlichen grund." In German mysticism, the "ground" is the deepest part of the soul where the human meets the Divine.</note> in yourself, through which you love **God’s** wisdom and wonder-works, along with your brothers. But as for why you hate yourself, you do that according to the **thine-hood**, in which evil clings to you; you do that because you would gladly break the **I-hood** <term>Ichtheit: The ego or "self-ness"; the state of being focused on one's own desires and separation from the Divine.</term> entirely within you, so that it would become a whole **Divine ground**. Love hates the **I-hood**, because the **I-hood** is a deadly thing, and the two may not well stand together: For **Love** possesses **Heaven** and dwells in itself: but **I-hood** possesses the **World** along with its nature, and also dwells in itself: Just as **Heaven** rules the **World**, and **Eternity** rules **Time**, so also **Love** rules over the natural life.

**The Disciple said:** Dear Master, tell me then, why must **Love** and **Suffering**, **Friend** and **Foe** stand together? Would it not be better if there were only **Love**?

**The Master said:** If **Love** did not stand in **suffering**, it would have nothing that it could love; but because its essence which it loves—namely, the poor soul—stands in suffering and pain, it has cause to love its own essence and to rescue it from pain, so that it may be loved in return. -># Of the Supersensual Life.<-

...be known what **Love** were, if she did not have that which she might love.

**The Disciple said:** What is **Love** in its power and virtue, and in its height and greatness?

**The Master said:** Its virtue is the **Nothing** <note>original: "das Nichts." For Böhme, the "Nothing" is the Divine "Unground"—a state of pure potential that exists before and beyond all created things, free from selfish desire.</note>, and its power is through **All**: its height is as high as **GOD**, and its greatness is greater than **GOD**; whoever finds it, finds nothing and all things.

**The Disciple said:** O dear Master, tell me then, how may I understand this?

**The Master said:** That which I said, that its virtue is the **Nothing**, you shall understand this: when you go out from all **creatures** <term>Creatur: A technical term in Böhme’s theology referring to any finite, created being or object that exists within the limits of time and nature.</term>, and become a "nothing" to all nature and creatures, then you are in the **eternal One**, which is **God** himself; thus you feel the highest virtue of **Love**.

But that which I said: Its power is through all—this you feel in your soul and body; when this great **Love** is kindled in you, it burns as no fire is able. You also see this in all the works of **God**, how **Love** has poured itself out into all things, and in all things is the innermost and outermost **ground** <note>original: "grund." The mystical foundation or "anchor" of a thing's existence, where it connects to the Divine.</note>: inwardly according to its power, and outwardly according to its form.

<summary>The Master explains the paradoxical nature of Divine Love as both "Nothing" (beyond the physical) and "All" (the power sustaining every creature). He teaches that by detaching from created things, one finds God, the "eternal One," within the soul.</summary>

<keywords>Jakob Böhme, Love, the Nothing, Creature, the Ground, Divine Power, Mysticism, The One, Spiritual Virtue</keywords>

-># Of the Supersensual Life.<-

And as I further said: its height is as high as **God**, this you understand within yourself, that it leads you into itself as high as **God** himself is: just as you can see this in our dear Lord **Christ** according to our humanity, which **Love** has led even into the highest throne, into the power of the **Godhead**.

But that I also spoke: its greatness were greater than **God**, that is also true; for where **God** does not dwell, there **Love** enters in: For when our dear Lord **Christ** stood in **Hell**, **Hell** was not **God**, but **Love** was there, and broke **Death**. Also when there is **Anguish** <note>original: "Angst." In Böhme's mystical system, *Angst* is the constricting, painful quality of the soul or nature when it is turned inward on itself, away from the light of God.</note>, **God** is not the anguish, but his **Love** is there, and leads you out of the anguish into **God**: when **God** hides himself in you, **Love** is there, and reveals him in you.

And that I further said: whoever finds it, finds **Nothing** and **All**, that is also true; for he finds a **supernatural**, **supersensual** **unground** <term>vngründ: A central concept in Böhme’s thought meaning "without ground" or "bottomless abyss." It refers to the primal, absolute source of all being that exists before and beyond created nature.</term>, where there is no place for its dwelling, and finds nothing that is its equal: Therefore one can compare it with nothing, for it is deeper than **Something** <note>original: "Ichts." A term Böhme coined as the opposite of "Nichts" (Nothing) to describe the world of manifested, finite things.</note>; therefore to all things it is as a **Nothing**, because it cannot be grasped: And because it is nothing, it is [freed] from all...

<summary>The Master explains that Love is "greater than God" because it reaches into the darkest places (like Hell or Anguish) where God's presence is hidden, and describes Love as a supernatural "Unground" that is deeper than all created things.</summary>

In Böhme’s thought, this means Christ is not just a concept, but a "substantial" presence within the soul of the believer.</note> in the faith of those who surrender themselves entirely to Him, and gives them His flesh as food and His blood as drink, and thus possesses the ground of their faith according to the inner nature <term>inner nature: "Inwendigkeit," the deep, internal spiritual reality of a person</term> of man. And whoever now withdraws his hand from such a suffering <note>original: "notleidenden," those experiencing distress or extreme poverty.</note> Christian person and will not serve him in his needs, he pushes Christ away from himself and despises Him in His members <note>Referring to the "Body of Christ," where every believer is a "member" or limb of the spiritual body.</note>. ...comforts—all of that is done to Christ Himself. The Disciple said: How then shall those endure on the Day of such Judgment, who so torment the poor wretched soul, and suck out his sweat <note>A vivid metaphor for exploiting someone’s hard labor for one's own gain.</note>, oppress him, and draw him to themselves with force, and regard him as their foot-rag <note>original: "Fußhader." The Master said: All of these do it to Christ Himself, and belong in His strict judgment. For they thus lay their hands upon Christ, persecute Him in His members <note>Referring to the "members" or parts of the spiritual Body of Christ.</note>, and alongside this they help the Devil increase his kingdom. And through such oppression, they draw the poor away from Christ, so that he <note>the poor person</note> also seeks a reckless way <note>original: "leichtfertigen Weg." Likely referring to the poor being driven to desperate or sinful acts, like theft, due to their mistreatment.</note> to fill his belly. Yes, they do nothing else than what the Devil himself does, who without ceasing resists the kingdom of Christ in love. All of these, if they do not convert to Christ with their whole heart and serve Him, must [go] into the hellish...

<meta>catchword: ...ish (continuation of "hellish")</meta>

<summary>This page discusses the spiritual consequences of social injustice, arguing that exploiting the poor is a direct assault on Christ Himself and serves the Devil's kingdom. It emphasizes the "Supersensual" unity of all believers as branches of a single tree (Christ).</summary>

-># Of the Supersensual Life.<-

-lish fire <note>The word "hellish" is completed from the previous page's catchword.</note>, where nothing but such Selfhood <note>original: "Eygenheit." **The Disciple said:** How then shall those endure, who in this time so struggle over the Kingdom of Christ, and for its sake persecute, shame, mock, and blaspheme one another?

**The Master said:** All of these have never yet known Christ; they exist only in the outward form <note>original: "Figur." Böhme uses this to mean a symbolic or external representation rather than the true inner reality.</note>, just as Heaven and Hell struggle with one another for victory. All rising up of pride, where people struggle only over opinions <note>original: "Meynungen." This refers to the ego's desire to possess truth or power for itself, treating spiritual matters as private property.</note>. For all Selfhood shall be given over to Darkness on the Day of Judgment; so too shall their useless quarreling, through which they seek no love, but only imaginary Selfhood—to make themselves seen in their opinions, and thereby cause the Princes to go to war for such imaginary opinions, and with their images storm and devastate land and people. -># Of the Supersensual Life.<-

...opinions <note>The word "opinions" follows from the previous page's discussion of sectarian dogmas.</note> cease / and all children of God will walk in the Love of Christ / and **HE** in us.

Everything that in this time of <term>Conflict</term> <note>original: "Streites." This refers to the "strife" or "struggle" of the material world, including the religious wars and the internal battle between the soul's light and dark properties.</note> / does not strive in the Spirit of Christ / and alone desires to promote Love / but seeks self-interest in Conflict / that is of the Devil / and belongs in the Darkness / and will be separated from Christ : For in Heaven everything serves God its Creator in humility.

**The Disciple said:** Why then does God let it happen in this time / that there is such Conflict?

**The Master said:** Life stands in Conflict / so that it may be made manifest / perceptible / findable / and so that Wisdom may be distinguished / and known : And it serves for the eternal joy of <term>Overcoming</term> <note>original: "Vberwindung." In Böhme's theology, the soul must actively triumph over its own dark, "natural" desires to reach the light.</note>. For in the Holy ones in Christ a great praise shall arise from it / that Christ has overcome the Darkness and all <term>Selfhood</term> <note>original: "Eygenheit." Thus God now lets all things stand in free will / so that the eternal dominion / according to Love and Wrath / according to Light and Darkness / may be made manifest and known / and each

<page-num>212</page-num>

# Of the Supersensual Life.

...every life causes and awakens its own <term>judgment</term> <note>original: "Vrtheil." Böhme suggests that judgment is not an external sentence handed down by a judge, but an internal state or "verdict" that the soul creates for itself based on its own nature.</note> within itself. For what is now a conflict and pain to the Saints in their misery, that shall be transformed for them into great joy: And what is a pleasure and joy to the godless in this world, that shall be turned into eternal pain and shame for them. And therefore, joy must arise for the Saints out of death, just as the light arises from the candle through the dying and consuming in the fire: So that life may thus become free of the <term>anguish</term> <note>original: "peinligkeit." This refers to the agonizing tension or "sting" of the material, natural state—the "fire" of existence—before it is transformed by the light.</note> of nature, and possess another world. Just as the light has a completely different <term>quality</term> <note>original: "Eygenschafft." A key term in Böhme’s writing, referring to the inherent nature or property of a thing.</note> than the fire; for the light gives of itself, but the fire takes and devours itself: Thus also the holy life of <term>gentleness</term> <note>original: "Sanfftmut." Often translated as meekness or mildness; in Böhme's system, it is the cooling, loving force of the "Light-world" that balances the "Fire-world" of the soul.</note> blossoms forth through death, where the self-will dies, and God's love-will alone governs and acts as All in All.

For thus has the Eternal **ONE** taken on perceptibility and <term>differentiation</term> <note>original: "Schiedligkeit." The process by which the unified divine light splits into distinct qualities, individual forms, or "separateness."</note>, and through death has led itself out with that perceptibility into a great kingdom of joy: So that there may be an eternal play in the infinite Unity, and an eternal cause for the kingdom of joy; therefore, the anguish must now be a ground and cause for such <term>motion</term> <note>original: "Bewegnuß." This suggests the dynamic activity, vibration, or "stirring" within the divine nature that prevents it from being a static void.</note>.

<meta>catchword: And</meta>

<summary>Böhme uses the metaphor of a candle flame to explain how spiritual joy is born from the "death" of the self-will and the "fire" of nature's anguish. He argues that the Eternal Unity assumes differentiation and suffering to create an eternal, joyful "play" of divine motion.</summary>

<keywords>transformation, light and fire, self-will, divine unity, differentiation, gentleness, joy, spiritual alchemy, Jacob Böhme</keywords>
